# Single-exponential recovery and bounded-price strictness for metric k-median

Coordinate `EN-114-000-0022-2-2026-PR-T4-UK-EX-M1-B-ML-H-0262` | `PM-125.1` | status ACTIVE | original slug `Single-Exponential-Recovery-and-Bounded-Price-Strictness-for-Metric-k-Median-September-24-2026` | date 2026-09-24 | derived from the openai/math preprint Single-Exponential-Recovery-and-Bounded-Price-Strictness-for-Metric-k-Median-September-24-2026, modified (Apache-2.0). Publication status: DRAFT.

```json
{
  "abstract_verbatim": "We give an exact-budget recovery algorithm for metric <i>k</i>-median with single-exponential dependence on the number of comparison clusters without accurate, distinct proxies in a supplied anchor solution. On positive integral metrics of polynomially bounded diameter, a sufficiently small total proxy error and logarithmically many such clusters yield a $`(1+2/e+\\varepsilon)`$ approximation in polynomial time with arbitrarily high success probability. We also prove bounded-price strictness for one compatible execution of the logarithmic-surplus construction. Together the recovery and payment arguments give a randomized $`(2-\\sigma)`$ approximation, for an absolute <i>σ</i> &gt; 0, on arbitrary finite rational metrics, both with high probability and in expectation, while opening at most <i>k</i> facilities on every output.",
```

<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give an exact-budget recovery algorithm for metric <i>k</i>-median with single-exponential dependence on the number of comparison clusters without accurate, distinct proxies in a supplied anchor solution. On positive integral metrics of polynomially bounded diameter, a sufficiently small total proxy error and logarithmically many such clusters yield a $`(1+2/e+\varepsilon)`$ approximation in polynomial time with arbitrarily high success probability. We also prove bounded-price strictness for one compatible execution of the logarithmic-surplus construction. Together the recovery and payment arguments give a randomized $`(2-\sigma)`$ approximation, for an absolute <i>σ</i> &gt; 0, on arbitrary finite rational metrics, both with high probability and in expectation, while opening at most <i>k</i> facilities on every output.

- **claim-refined-anchor-recovery** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-ref-recovery)): Let $D$ and $F$ be disjoint indexed sets in a metric whose distances between distinct indices are positive integers bounded by a fixed polynomial in $N=\|D\|+\|F\|$. Fix $\varepsilon>0$ and $0<L_0<\infty$, and choose a fixed rational parameter $$0<\mu\le \min\{1/4,\varepsilon/12\}.$$ Let $S\subseteq F$ have cardinality $h\ge1$. Suppose there exists a comparison solution $O\subseteq F$ of cardinality $h$ and cost $P>0$, with a partition into good and bad centers and distinct proxies $\phi_i\in S$ for the good centers, such that $$\label{ref:proxy-hypothesis} \sum_{i\ \mathrm{good}}\sum_{p\in C_i}d(p,\phi_i) \le P_g+\mu P, \qquad m:=\|\{i:i\ \mathrm{bad}\}\|\le L_0\log N.$$ Given $S$, $\mu$, $L_0$, and a failure parameter $\zeta\in(0,1)$, a randomized algorithm returns $\widehat S\subseteq F$ with $$\|\widehat S\|\le h \quad\text{always},\qquad \operatorname{cost}(\widehat S) \le (1+2/e+\varepsilon)P \quad\text{with probability at least }1-\zeta.$$ Its running time is polynomial in $N$ and $\log(1/\zeta)$ for the fixed parameters. The algorithm is not given $O$, $P$, the partition, or its proxies.
  - Explanation: Recovers a feasible facility set from the supplied anchor under accurate distinct proxies and a logarithmic exceptional-cluster bound. The comparison solution and its certificate are used only in the analysis.
- **claim-global-budgeted-approximation** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-main-theorem)): There is an absolute constant $\sigma>0$ with the following property. For every fixed $a>0$, a randomized polynomial-time algorithm for metric $k$-median always returns a set $S\subseteq F$ with $\|S\|\le k$ and satisfies $$\Pr\!\left\{\operatorname{cost}(S) \le(2-\sigma)\operatorname{opt}_k\right\} \ge 1-(\|D\|+\|F\|+2)^{-a}.$$ The same algorithm can be chosen to satisfy $\mathbb E\operatorname{cost}(S)\le(2-\sigma)\operatorname{opt}_k$. The running time is polynomial in the bit length of the finite rational metric input. Its degree may depend on the fixed constants.
  - Explanation: Gives a randomized approximation strictly below the stated barrier on finite rational metrics, with feasibility on every output and guarantees in probability and expectation.
- **claim-metric-rounding-transfer** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-pre-rounding)): Fix rational $0<\varepsilon_0\le1$. Suppose a polynomial-time algorithm for the bounded integral instances just described always returns at most $k$ facilities of cost at most $5\operatorname{opt}_k$, and, with probability at least $1-p$, returns cost at most $A\operatorname{opt}_k$, where $A\le5$. Then a randomized polynomial-time algorithm for arbitrary finite rational metric instances always returns at most $k$ facilities of cost at most $5(1+\varepsilon_0)\operatorname{opt}_k$ and, with probability at least $1-p$, returns cost at most $A(1+\varepsilon_0)\operatorname{opt}_k$. The bounded integral distances needed are at most $\lceil20n^2/\varepsilon_0\rceil+1$.
  - Explanation: Transfers the integral-instance algorithm to rational metrics while preserving the facility budget and controlling both the unconditional and successful-event costs.
- **claim-exact-local-descent** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-loc-descent)): Suppose all distances are integers at most $B$, where $B$ is bounded by a fixed polynomial in $N=n+\|F\|$. From any nonempty initial set of $r$ facilities, a width-$5$ local optimum of size $r$ can be found in polynomial time, with no increase in cost.
  - Explanation: Strict improving exchanges terminate in polynomial time because the integral objective has a polynomially bounded range.
- **claim-protected-weighted-swaps** (bound, [source](#stmt-loc-swap)): Under the preceding hypotheses, if $S$ is a width-$5$ local optimum and $u=6/5$, then $$S_b+uS_g\le (1+2u)P_b+uD_g. \label{loc:strong-swap}$$ Consequently, $$\operatorname{cost}(S)\le uD_g+(1+2u)P_b. \label{loc:swap-bound}$$ In particular, for any comparison set $O$ of size at most $\|S\|$, $\operatorname{cost}(S)\le(17/5)P\le5P$.
  - Explanation: Protecting distinct proxies during the exchange analysis bounds local cost through proxy assignment cost and bad-cluster cost. Declaring all comparison centers bad gives the fallback guarantee.
- **claim-separated-cluster-anchoring** (construction, [source](#stmt-loc-anchoring)): Let $S$ be a width-$5$ local optimum, and let $O$ be a comparison set with $2\le h=\|O\|\le\|S\|$ and $P=\operatorname{cost}(O)>0$. Suppose $b>0$ satisfies $$b\left(\ell+ \bigl\|\{i\in O:n_i\operatorname{sep}_i<b\}\bigr\|\right) \le10P. \label{loc:scale-condition}$$ For every fixed $\mu\in(0,1]$, there is a partition $O=O_g\mathbin{\dot\cup}O_b$ and distinct proxies $\phi_i\in S$ for $i\in O_g$ such that, with $m=\|O_b\|$, $$\sum_{i\in O_g}\sum_{p\in C_i}d(p,\phi_i) \le P_g+\mu P, \qquad m\le C_\mu\frac{P}{b}, \label{loc:anchoring-conclusion}$$ where the finite constant $$J_\mu=\left\lceil\frac{40}{\mu}\right\rceil, \qquad C_\mu=10+\frac{600}{\mu}\,2^{J_\mu-1} \label{loc:anchoring-constant}$$ depends only on $\mu$.
  - Explanation: The scale condition yields an existential partition with accurate distinct proxies and a bounded exceptional count. Finding this partition is unnecessary for the recovery algorithm.
- **claim-population-radius-computation** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-sam-computable-radius)): If $d_1\le\cdots\le d_n$ are the client distances from $w$, including the zero distance from $w$ to itself, then $$r^0(w)=\min_{1\le j\le n}\max\{d_j,P/(mj)\}>0. \label{sam:r0-formula}$$ In particular the minimum in Equation <a href="#sam:r0-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sam:r0-definition">[sam:r0-definition]</a> is attained and its closed ball satisfies the defining population inequality. Suppose that $A\subseteq\mathcal B$ and each $i\in A$ has a client $w_i\in C_i$ with $r_i=d(w_i,i)\le a\bar d_i$, where $a>0$. Then $$\begin{aligned} r^0(w_i)&\le(a+2)\bar d_i+\frac{2P}{mn_i},\\ \sum_{i\in A}n_i r^0(w_i) &\le(a+2)\sum_{i\in A}P_i+\frac{2\|A\|}{m}P \le(a+4)P. \end{aligned} \label{sam:leader-radius-sum}$$ The last bound is $5.2P$ when $a=1.2$, and is at most $5.5P$ when $a\le3/2$.
  - Explanation: Computes the attained population radius from sorted client distances and bounds its aggregate contribution for leaders from distinct bad clusters.
- **claim-leaf-metric-and-assignment-monotonicity** (construction, [source](#stmt-sam-dummy-metric)): The construction above extends the original metric without changing its distances. For every original point $x$, $$d(x,\delta_i)=d(x,w_i)+R_i,\qquad d(\delta_\infty,\delta_i)=\Delta+R_i.$$ If $R_i\ge d(w_i,O)$, then $d(x,\delta_i)\ge d(x,O)$ for every original point $x$. If $x_i\in\mathcal F_i$, then $d(x,x_i)\le d(x,\delta_i)$ for every original point $x$. Every nonempty real solution similarly dominates the permanent dummy.* *There is a fixed assignment rule, independent of $O,S_0$, and $Q$, such that after any state in which further operations only add leaves, the client set assigned to any fixed subset of surviving original centers can only shrink.
  - Explanation: Leaf extensions preserve original distances and give domination inequalities for completion. A fixed tie order makes assignments to surviving original centers shrink during leaf additions.
- **claim-amortized-radius-menu-budget** (bound, [source](#stmt-sam-radius-budget)): Fix $0<\kappa\le1$. Consider a sequence with at most $m$ ball additions, each using the prescribed correct radius above, and suppose that every previously added leaf remains present at every later addition. Arbitrary removals of original centers may occur between additions. Among additions using the third prescribed menu, the closed client balls $\mathcal D(w,r^0(w))$ are pairwise disjoint. Writing $r=d(w,O)$ and $q=r^0(w)$ for these additions, one has $$\sum_{\text{third menu}}\frac rq\le\frac{10m}{9}, \qquad \sum_{\text{third menu}}j \le\left(\frac{10}{9\kappa}+1\right)m. \label{sam:third-menu-budget}$$ Consequently each prescribed third-menu index is at most $J_{\kappa,m}$. There is a constant $c_\kappa\in(0,1]$ such that the product of the probabilities of all prescribed menu-and-entry choices is at least $$c_\kappa^m\, 2^{-(10/(9\kappa)+1)m}=\exp(-O_\kappa(m)). \label{sam:menu-probability}$$ These statements do not require a fixed order of additions or a fixed rule for selecting their leaders.
  - Explanation: Persistent leaves force disjointness of the costly client balls. Their comparison-cost charges bound the total menu-index loss over an adaptive sequence.
- **claim-adaptive-guide-likelihood** (bound, [source](#stmt-sam-probability)): Fix $O,P,m$, $0<\kappa\le1$, and a number $\rho\in(0,1]$ independent of the instance size. Suppose an analytical guided process uses the same initial state and, at each of its nonterminal histories, does the following:* 1. *From that history it deterministically specifies ball mode or removal mode and a set of eligible clients such that, under the actual weighted client draw and fair mode choice, the joint probability of this event is at least $\rho$.* 2. *It draws the client from the actual law conditioned on that event. It performs exactly the actual update for the sampled outcome. Thus a removal deletes the original reference center assigned to that client, which must be a previously unremoved member of $S_0$. A ball addition has a leader in a previously uncovered bad cluster and uses its prescribed correct menu and entry.* *Suppose the guide stops after at most $2m$ steps on every supported trajectory. Leaves are retained throughout, and there are at most $m$ ball additions and $m$ removals. The eligible sets, modes, and stopping rules may depend on the entire preceding augmented history and on $O$; they may also have several successive stages, with their stage determined by that history. Then the actual experiment records a terminal state of this guide with probability at least $$\rho^{2m}c_\kappa^m 2^{-(10/(9\kappa)+1)m} =\exp(-O_{\kappa,\rho}(m)). \label{sam:guided-likelihood}$$ The same conclusion, with a changed constant in the exponent, holds with a polynomial-time implementation using a bounded number of random bits per operation.
  - Explanation: Summing disjoint terminal-history cylinders transfers the guided process probability to the blind experiment. The argument accommodates adaptive stages and finite-bit sampling.
- **claim-near-client-witness-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-sam-near-clients)): Let $n_i>0$, $a>1$, and $N_i(a)=\{p\in C_i:d_p^*\le a\bar d_i\}$. For any nonempty reference set in a metric extension, set $T_i(a)=\sum_{p\in N_i(a)}d(p,H)$. Then $$\|N_i(a)\|\ge(1-1/a)n_i,\qquad d(i,H)\le a\bar d_i+ \frac{T_i(a)}{(1-1/a)n_i}. \label{sam:general-near-bound}$$ For the main cutoff $N_i=N_i(1.2)$ and $T_i=\sum_{p\in N_i}d(p,H)$ one also has $$d(i,H)\le1.02\bar d_i+\frac{60T_i}{n_i},\qquad d(p,H)\le1.85d_p^*+\frac{60T_i}{n_i}\quad(p\in C_i\setminus N_i). \label{sam:far-client-bound}$$
  - Explanation: Averaging over near clients and applying the triangle inequality bounds center-reference distances and the distances of clients outside the main near set.
- **claim-sampling-progress-mass** (bound, [source](#stmt-sam-sampling-mass)): At every correct main state with $\kappa=10^{-6}$, $$W(H)\le3.42P+61T_{\rm near}(H). \label{sam:weight-bound}$$ If the first stopping inequality fails, the joint event of sampling a client in $\bigcup_{i\in B}N_i$ and choosing ball mode has probability at least $$\rho_\kappa=\frac{\kappa}{2(3.42+61\kappa)}>0. \label{sam:progress-constant}$$ If the first stopping inequality holds and the second fails, the same lower bound holds for sampling a client assigned to $R^*$ and choosing removal mode.
  - Explanation: A decomposition by good, covered bad, and uncovered bad clients bounds total sampling weight. Failure of either stopping condition consequently supplies positive progress probability.
- **claim-successful-recorded-prefix** (construction, [source](#stmt-sam-successful-prefix)): Assume the equal-size comparison setup and Equation <a href="#sam:proxy-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sam:proxy-hypothesis">[sam:proxy-hypothesis]</a>, with $1\le m\le M_0$, and use $\kappa=10^{-6}$. Given $S$ and the correct guesses $P,m$, one run of the actual experiment has, with probability at least $\exp(-C_\kappa m)$ for an absolute constant $C_\kappa<\infty$, a recorded prefix with the following properties:* 1. *$Q\subseteq S_0$, and the balls correspond to distinct $i\in A\subseteq\mathcal B$, one per such cluster, with $w_i\in N_i$ and $$r_i=d(w_i,i)=d(w_i,O)\le R_i \le(1+\kappa)r_i+\kappa r^0(w_i).$$ Consequently $i\in\mathcal F_i$ and $\sum_{i\in A}n_i r^0(w_i)\le5.2P$.* 2. *For $H$ in Equation <a href="#sam:reference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sam:reference">[sam:reference]</a>, $B=\mathcal B\setminus A$, $R^*=S_0\setminus Q$, and the nearest assignment sets $J_s$, $$\sum_{i\in B}\sum_{p\in N_i}d(p,H)\le\kappa P, \qquad \sum_{s\in R^*}\sum_{p\in J_s}d(p,H)\le\kappa P.$$* 3. *The reference retains every good proxy. The covered-client bound in Equation <a href="#sam:covered-client-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sam:covered-client-bound">[sam:covered-client-bound]</a> holds, and for each uncovered nonempty cluster the far-client bound in Equation <a href="#sam:far-client-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sam:far-client-bound">[sam:far-client-bound]</a> holds with $T_i=\sum_{p\in N_i}d(p,H)$.* *The run has at most $2m+1$ recorded prefixes and takes polynomial time for fixed $\kappa$, with rational numbers of polynomial bit length. Neither its input nor its stopping decisions require $O$, its partition, the proxies, or $S_0$.
  - Explanation: The blind experiment records a prefix with protected proxies, correct balls, and small stopping quantities with single-exponential probability. Comparison-dependent stopping decisions remain analytical.
- **claim-categorical-submodular-maximization** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-comp-partition-max)): Let $g$ be a normalized, monotone, submodular function given by an exact value oracle, and let $E$ have a partition into $a$ parts. For every $0<\varepsilon<1-1/e$, a randomized algorithm uses $\operatorname{poly}(\|E\|,a,1/\varepsilon)$ oracle calls and returns a feasible $X$ satisfying $$\mathbb E[g(X)]\ge(1-1/e-\varepsilon) \max\{g(Y): \|Y\cap E_j\|\le1\text{ for all }j\}.$$ Every random draw can be made using a bounded polynomial number of fair bits. If the parts are nonempty, filling unoccupied parts preserves the guarantee. In particular, one obtains the factor $0.62$; more generally, for every fixed rational $\theta>1/e$ with $\theta<1$, one obtains expected gain at least $1-\theta$ times the maximum.
  - Explanation: A finite categorical continuous-greedy procedure obtains the expected partition-constrained gain using bounded random bits. Filling unused nonempty parts preserves the guarantee.
- **claim-surrogate-realization-and-savings** (construction, [source](#stmt-comp-surrogate-properties)): For every finite branch and feasible $X$, one can construct a nonempty real solution $U_X\subseteq F$ with $\|U_X\|\le h$ and $\operatorname{cost}(U_X)\le\Psi(X)$. Moreover $g(X)=\Psi(\varnothing)-\Psi(X)$ is normalized, nonnegative, monotone, and submodular on all subsets of $E$.
  - Explanation: Every feasible surrogate choice can be realized by a nonempty real solution within budget. Its savings function is monotone and submodular, including the optimized red-removal contribution.
- **claim-prefix-completion-with-slack** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-comp-completion)): In the prefix setting above, fix a rational $1/e<\theta<1$. Define $$R^*=S_0\setminus Q,\qquad J=\bigcup_{s\in R^*}J_s,\qquad I=\sum_{p\in J}d(p,H),\qquad X^*=\{(i,i):i\in A\}.$$ For $p\in C_i$, let $$c_p=\begin{cases} d(p,\phi_i),&i\text{ good},\\ d_p^*+\theta(r_i+R_i),&i\in A,\\ d_p^*,&i\in B, \end{cases} \qquad b_p=\theta d(p,H)+(1-\theta)\min\{d(p,H),d(p,X^*)\}. \label{comp:client-charges}$$ There is an algorithm, using only $S,m,Q$ and the given balls, whose outputs always have at most $h$ real facilities and whose work is $\exp(O(m))\operatorname{poly}(N,1/(\theta-1/e))$, with polynomial dependence also on the input bit length. There is an event in its color and label choices of probability at least $\exp(-O(m))$ such that, for every realization of those choices in the event, its output $U$ satisfies $$\mathbb E[\operatorname{cost}(U)] \le 2I+\sum_{p\in J}c_p+\sum_{p\notin J}b_p. \label{comp:completion-bound}$$ The expectation is over the remaining optimization randomness. For good clients $b_p\le c_p$; for clients of $A$ also $b_p\le c_p$; for clients of $B$ one always has $b_p\le d(p,H)$. No upper bound on $\operatorname{cost}(H)$, proxy error, or ball radii is needed for this theorem beyond containment of $i$ in its designated ball.
  - Explanation: Combines exact blue-destination optimization with submodular red and ball choices. A favorable color-and-label event yields the conditional expected charge bound while every output respects the facility budget.
- **claim-single-exponential-recovery** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-comp-recovery)): Suppose $\|S\|=\|O\|=h\ge1$, $P=\operatorname{cost}(O)>0$, and $O$ has an injective proxy map into $S$ for its good centers such that $$\sum_{i\text{ good}}\sum_{p\in C_i}d(p,\phi_i) \le P_g+0.01P.$$ Assume the number $m$ of bad centers is at most a supplied bound $M_0$. On the positive bounded integral instances used in the body of the proof, for every $0<\zeta<1$ an algorithm knowing only $S,M_0,\zeta$ returns a real solution of size at most $h$ and cost at most $1.97P$ with probability at least $1-\zeta$. Its running time is $$\exp(O(M_0))\operatorname{poly}(N)\log(2/\zeta).$$ In particular this is polynomial time for every fixed constant in $M_0=O(\log N)$. Local optimality of $S$ is not an additional hypothesis of this theorem.
  - Explanation: Amplifies successful sampling and completion trials under the supplied exceptional-count bound. Local optimality is unnecessary once the proxy promise holds.
- **claim-two-stage-sampling-endpoint** (construction, [source](#stmt-ref-two-stage)): The blind sampling procedure, run for at most $2m$ steps and retaining all prefixes, has probability at least $\exp(-O_\mu(m))$ of producing a prefix with the following properties.* *There is an earlier prefix $H^0$, with covered set $A^1$ and remaining bad set $B^0$, at which the deleted set is the final set $Q$. Put $$R^*=S_0\setminus Q,\qquad J^0=\bigcup_{s\in R^*}J_s(H^0),\qquad T_i=\sum_{p\in N_i^1}d(p,H^0)\quad(i\in B^0).$$ Then $$\label{ref:first-stop} \sum_{i\in B^0}T_i\le\kappa P, \qquad \sum_{p\in J^0}d(p,H^0)\le\kappa P.$$ For each nonempty cluster $i\in B^0$, define $f_i=\|C_i\cap J^0\|/n_i$. All balls added after $H^0$ correspond to a set $A^2\subseteq\{i\in B^0:n_i>0,\ f_i<1/3\}$, and their leaders lie in $N_i^2$; the leaders for $A^1$ lie in $N_i^1$. At the final prefix, set $A=A^1\cup A^2$, $B=B^0\setminus A^2$, and $J=\bigcup_{s\in R^*}J_s(H)$. We have $$\label{ref:second-stop} J\subseteq J^0,\qquad \sum_{p\in J}d(p,H)\le\kappa P,\qquad \sum_{\substack{i\in B:n_i>0\\ f_i<1/3}} \sum_{p\in N_i^2}d(p,H)\le\kappa P.$$ Every ball satisfies Equation <a href="#ref:radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ref:radius">[ref:radius]</a>, and the collection satisfies $$\label{ref:radius-sum} \sum_{i\in A}n_i r^0(w_i)\le\frac{11}{2}P.$$ The earlier prefix and the conditions defining it need not be recognized by the algorithm.
  - Explanation: A staged analytical guide first controls removal assignments and primary near-client cost, then adds balls only to eligible remaining clusters. Persistent leaves and fixed ties preserve the required nested assignment sets.
- **claim-refined-completion-charges** (bound, [source](#stmt-ref-charges)): For the endpoint above, the cost bound in Equation <a href="#ref:completion-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ref:completion-bound">[ref:completion-bound]</a> is at most $$\label{ref:explicit-bound} \left(1+2\theta a_1+\mu+11\kappa+\frac\kappa q\right)P \le (1+2/e+6\mu)P.$$
  - Explanation: Separating the covered stages and the remaining clusters retains the first-stop assignment fractions. Charging shared radius and near-client errors only once gives the refined recovery coefficient.
- **claim-surplus-construction-interface** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-imp-cglss)): For the metric range above and fixed $v$, the construction underlying Theorem 1.2 of (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson 2026) [[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)] can be run in polynomial time at any target $2\le h\le \|F\|$. With precision $\eta=2^{-N}$, its output has one of the following forms.* 1. *A solution of $\eta$-valid sequences with exactly $h$ distinct regular facilities and at most $L=O_v(\log N)$ free copies, for some final price $f>0$ and specified final copy lengths.* 2. *A solution of $0$-valid sequences containing only regular facilities, at price $f=N^{-2}$, with at most $h$ facilities.* *One common integer $L$ can be used for all targets on an instance. For every regular opening, the algorithm also returns its witness set and certified bids.
  - Explanation: The imported construction supplies either a normal valid-sequence solution with bounded free surplus or a small-price regular-only solution. Witness sets and certified bids accompany regular openings.
- **claim-compatible-final-sequence-replay** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-imp-surplus-history)): Given valid sequences of either output form in Theorem <a href="#imp:cglss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:cglss">20</a>, with their price, fixed copy lengths, opening witnesses and certified bids, a polynomial-time replay produces final budgets $(\alpha_p)_{p\in D}$ and a real solution $U\subseteq F$ of size at most $h+L$ with the following properties.* *The history replays the *final* sequences using their fixed final price and fixed final copy lengths. Each atomic step opens one facility or performs one simultaneous phase update. Its actual pre-step and post-step open sets satisfy $Q_t^-\subseteq Q_t^+$, and these sets accumulate throughout the history. We call a client’s transition to inactivity its removal from the active set. Let $t(p)$ be the step at which $p$ becomes inactive and $z_p$ the phase value immediately *before* that step. At this pre-step state all active clients have budget $z_p$, and $$\begin{aligned} z_p&\le(1-\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^-),\label{imp:active-threshold}\\ \alpha_p&\le Kz_p,\label{imp:final-cap}\\ (1-\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^+)&\le\alpha_p\le Kz_p. \label{imp:post-removal} \end{aligned}$$ The distance on the left of <a href="#imp:post-removal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:post-removal">[imp:post-removal]</a> cannot increase at any later step. If $I_t$ is the actual inactive set just before step $t=t(p)$, then, for every regular facility $i\in F$, $$\sum_{j\notin I_t}[z_p-d(i,j)]^+ +\sum_{j\in I_t}[(1-\delta)d(j,Q_t^-)-d(i,j)]^+ \le\lambda. \label{imp:no-overbid}$$ For these same final budgets, the dual inequality is $$\sum_{p\in D}[\alpha_p-2d(i,p)]^+\le\lambda \qquad(i\in F). \label{imp:dual}$$* *In the normal branch the history opens exactly $h$ paid regular facilities before projecting free copies, and $$(1-\delta)\operatorname{cost}(U)+h(\lambda-N\eta) \le\sum_{p\in D}\alpha_p, \qquad \eta=2^{-N}. \label{imp:payment}$$ In the small-price branch, $\|U\|\le h$, $\lambda=2/N^2$, $\eta=0$, and, writing $r=\|U\|$, the stronger exact-payment inequality is $$(1-\delta)\operatorname{cost}(U)+r\lambda \le\sum_{p\in D}\alpha_p. \label{imp:small-payment}$$ In particular its paid term can be dropped. For every nonempty real comparison solution $H\subseteq F$ one also has $$\sum_{p\in D}\alpha_p \le \|H\|\lambda+2\operatorname{cost}(H). \label{imp:comparison}$$
  - Explanation: Replaying the final sequences at their fixed price and copy lengths produces compatible budgets for payment, no-overbidding, and final dual bounds. Projection of free copies gives a real solution with bounded surplus.
- **claim-bounded-price-strictness** (bound, [source](#stmt-str-strictness)): For $T>0$ define $$\label{str:constants} B=T+2,\qquad \rho=\frac{1}{200B},\qquad c(T)=\frac{\rho^2}{100}>0.$$ Assume $0\le\delta<1$, $0<v\le1$, and $$\label{str:v-condition} v^2\le\frac{c(T)}{6(T+2)}.$$ For any regular facility $i\in F$ and nonempty client set $C\subseteq D$ of positive cost $P_C=\sum_{p\in C}d(i,p)$, the history in Lemma <a href="#imp:surplus-history" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:surplus-history">21</a> satisfies $$\label{str:conclusion} \lambda\le TP_C \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \sum_{p\in C}\alpha_p \le \lambda+\left(2-\frac{c(T)}2\right)P_C.$$ The set $C$ need not be a cluster of the algorithm or of an optimal solution.
  - Explanation: A suffix-maximum argument and complementary slack bounds improve the cluster budget estimate when price is bounded relative to positive cluster cost. The full opening payment is retained.
- **claim-low-sensitivity-candidate** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-sur-low-sensitivity)): With these fixed parameters, suppose $k>2L$ and $$\label{sur:sensitivity} \operatorname{opt}_{k-2L}\le(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ Put $h=k-L$. In polynomial time one can compute a real facility set $S$ of size $k$ with $$\label{sur:conclusion} \operatorname{cost}(S) \le\left(2-\frac{c}{100}\right)\operatorname{opt}_h \le\left(2-\frac{c}{100}\right)(1+\xi) \operatorname{opt}_k.$$ The algorithm does not need to test Equation <a href="#sur:sensitivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:sensitivity">[sur:sensitivity]</a>.
  - Explanation: The surplus solution leaves room within the final budget. Local anchoring and compatible strict payment accounting improve its cost without testing the optimum-cost sensitivity condition.
- **claim-sensitive-cardinality-separation** (construction, [source](#stmt-asm-sensitive)): Suppose $k>2L$, $0<\xi\le1$, and $$\operatorname{opt}_{k-2L}>(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ Put $\beta=\xi/(4L)$. There is an integer $h'$ with $k-2L<h'\le k$ such that $$\label{asm:sensitive-ratios} \operatorname{opt}_{h'-1}\ge(1+\beta)\operatorname{opt}_{h'}, \qquad \operatorname{opt}_{h'}\le(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ For an optimum $O$ of size $h'$, of cost $P=\operatorname{opt}_{h'}$, every comparison cluster satisfies $$\label{asm:separation} n_i\operatorname{sep}_i\ge\beta P, \qquad \operatorname{sep}_i=d(i,O\setminus\{i\}).$$
  - Explanation: A product of consecutive optimum ratios identifies a nearby sensitive cardinality. Rerouting a deleted cluster then forces separation and excludes empty clusters at that optimum.
- **claim-integral-instance-candidate-selection** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-asm-integral)): With the constants above, for every $0<\zeta<1$ there is a randomized algorithm which always returns at most $k$ facilities and cost at most $5\operatorname{opt}_k$, and with probability at least $1-\zeta$ returns cost at most $$\label{asm:integral-guarantee} (2-\gamma)(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ Its running time is polynomial in $N$ and $\log(2/\zeta)$ for the fixed constants and the fixed polynomial distance bound.
  - Explanation: Computes candidates from both sensitivity regimes and keeps the cheapest feasible one. Retaining the local-search candidate bounds cost even when randomized recovery fails.

```json
        "exact_quote": "If $d_1\\le\\cdots\\le d_n$ are the client distances from $w$, including the zero distance from $w$ to itself, then $$r^0(w)=\\min_{1\\le j\\le n}\\max\\{d_j,P/(mj)\\}>0.\n \\label{sam:r0-formula}$$ In particular the minimum in Equation <a href=\"#sam:r0-definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"sam:r0-definition\">[sam:r0-definition]</a> is attained and its closed ball satisfies the defining population inequality. Suppose that $A\\subseteq\\mathcal B$ and each $i\\in A$ has a client $w_i\\in C_i$ with $r_i=d(w_i,i)\\le a\\bar d_i$, where $a>0$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n r^0(w_i)&\\le(a+2)\\bar d_i+\\frac{2P}{mn_i},\\\\\n \\sum_{i\\in A}n_i r^0(w_i)\n &\\le(a+2)\\sum_{i\\in A}P_i+\\frac{2|A|}{m}P\n \\le(a+4)P.\n \\end{aligned}\n \\label{sam:leader-radius-sum}$$ The last bound is $5.2P$ when $a=1.2$, and is at most $5.5P$ when $a\\le3/2$.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-sam-computable-radius",
          "exact_quote": "If $d_1\\le\\cdots\\le d_n$ are the client distances from $w$, including the zero distance from $w$ to itself, then $$r^0(w)=\\min_{1\\le j\\le n}\\max\\{d_j,P/(mj)\\}>0.\n \\label{sam:r0-formula}$$ In particular the minimum in Equation <a href=\"#sam:r0-definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"sam:r0-definition\">[sam:r0-definition]</a> is attained and its closed ball satisfies the defining population inequality. Suppose that $A\\subseteq\\mathcal B$ and each $i\\in A$ has a client $w_i\\in C_i$ with $r_i=d(w_i,i)\\le a\\bar d_i$, where $a>0$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n r^0(w_i)&\\le(a+2)\\bar d_i+\\frac{2P}{mn_i},\\\\\n \\sum_{i\\in A}n_i r^0(w_i)\n &\\le(a+2)\\sum_{i\\in A}P_i+\\frac{2|A|}{m}P\n \\le(a+4)P.\n \\end{aligned}\n \\label{sam:leader-radius-sum}$$ The last bound is $5.2P$ when $a=1.2$, and is at most $5.5P$ when $a\\le3/2$.",
          "source_id": "PM-125.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "If $d_1\\le\\cdots\\le d_n$ are the client distances from $w$, including the zero distance from $w$ to itself, then $$r^0(w)=\\min_{1\\le j\\le n}\\max\\{d_j,P/(mj)\\}>0.\n \\label{sam:r0-formula}$$ In particular the minimum in Equation <a href=\"#sam:r0-definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"sam:r0-definition\">[sam:r0-definition]</a> is attained and its closed ball satisfies the defining population inequality.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sam-dummy-metric",
        "exact_quote": "The construction above extends the original metric without changing its distances. For every original point $x$, $$d(x,\\delta_i)=d(x,w_i)+R_i,\\qquad\n d(\\delta_\\infty,\\delta_i)=\\Delta+R_i.$$ If $R_i\\ge d(w_i,O)$, then $d(x,\\delta_i)\\ge d(x,O)$ for every original point $x$. If $x_i\\in\\mathcal F_i$, then $d(x,x_i)\\le d(x,\\delta_i)$ for every original point $x$. Every nonempty real solution similarly dominates the permanent dummy.*\n\n*There is a fixed assignment rule, independent of $O,S_0$, and $Q$, such that after any state in which further operations only add leaves, the client set assigned to any fixed subset of surviving original centers can only shrink.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sam-radius-budget",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $0<\\kappa\\le1$. Consider a sequence with at most $m$ ball additions, each using the prescribed correct radius above, and suppose that every previously added leaf remains present at every later addition. Arbitrary removals of original centers may occur between additions. Among additions using the third prescribed menu, the closed client balls $\\mathcal D(w,r^0(w))$ are pairwise disjoint. Writing $r=d(w,O)$ and $q=r^0(w)$ for these additions, one has $$\\sum_{\\text{third menu}}\\frac rq\\le\\frac{10m}{9},\n \\qquad\n \\sum_{\\text{third menu}}j\n \\le\\left(\\frac{10}{9\\kappa}+1\\right)m.\n \\label{sam:third-menu-budget}$$ Consequently each prescribed third-menu index is at most $J_{\\kappa,m}$. There is a constant $c_\\kappa\\in(0,1]$ such that the product of the probabilities of all prescribed menu-and-entry choices is at least $$c_\\kappa^m\\,\n 2^{-(10/(9\\kappa)+1)m}=\\exp(-O_\\kappa(m)).\n \\label{sam:menu-probability}$$ These statements do not require a fixed order of additions or a fixed rule for selecting their leaders.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-sam-radius-budget",

There is a constant $c_\\kappa\\in(0,1]$ such that the product of the probabilities of all prescribed menu-and-entry choices is at least $$c_\\kappa^m\\,\n 2^{-(10/(9\\kappa)+1)m}=\\exp(-O_\\kappa(m)).\n \\label{sam:menu-probability}$$ These statements do not require a fixed order of additions or a fixed rule for selecting their leaders.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sam-probability",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $O,P,m$, $0<\\kappa\\le1$, and a number $\\rho\\in(0,1]$ independent of the instance size. Suppose an analytical guided process uses the same initial state and, at each of its nonterminal histories, does the following:*\n\n1.  *From that history it deterministically specifies ball mode or removal mode and a set of eligible clients such that, under the actual weighted client draw and fair mode choice, the joint probability of this event is at least $\\rho$.*\n\n2.  *It draws the client from the actual law conditioned on that event. It performs exactly the actual update for the sampled outcome. Thus a removal deletes the original reference center assigned to that client, which must be a previously unremoved member of $S_0$. A ball addition has a leader in a previously uncovered bad cluster and uses its prescribed correct menu and entry.*\n\n\n*Suppose the guide stops after at most $2m$ steps on every supported trajectory. Leaves are retained throughout, and there are at most $m$ ball additions and $m$ removals. The eligible sets, modes, and stopping rules may depend on the entire preceding augmented history and on $O$; they may also have several successive stages, with their stage determined by that history. Then the actual experiment records a terminal state of this guide with probability at least $$\\rho^{2m}c_\\kappa^m\n       2^{-(10/(9\\kappa)+1)m}\n =\\exp(-O_{\\kappa,\\rho}(m)).\n \\label{sam:guided-likelihood}$$ The same conclusion, with a changed constant in the exponent, holds with a polynomial-time implementation using a bounded number of random bits per operation.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },

        {

Leaves are retained throughout, and there are at most $m$ ball additions and $m$ removals. The eligible sets, modes, and stopping rules may depend on the entire preceding augmented history and on $O$; they may also have several successive stages, with their stage determined by that history. Then the actual experiment records a terminal state of this guide with probability at least $$\\rho^{2m}c_\\kappa^m\n       2^{-(10/(9\\kappa)+1)m}\n =\\exp(-O_{\\kappa,\\rho}(m)).\n \\label{sam:guided-likelihood}$$ The same conclusion, with a changed constant in the exponent, holds with a polynomial-time implementation using a bounded number of random bits per operation.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sam-near-clients",
        "exact_quote": "Let $n_i>0$, $a>1$, and $N_i(a)=\\{p\\in C_i:d_p^*\\le a\\bar d_i\\}$. For any nonempty reference set in a metric extension, set $T_i(a)=\\sum_{p\\in N_i(a)}d(p,H)$. Then $$|N_i(a)|\\ge(1-1/a)n_i,\\qquad\n d(i,H)\\le a\\bar d_i+\n              \\frac{T_i(a)}{(1-1/a)n_i}.\n \\label{sam:general-near-bound}$$ For the main cutoff $N_i=N_i(1.2)$ and $T_i=\\sum_{p\\in N_i}d(p,H)$ one also has $$d(i,H)\\le1.02\\bar d_i+\\frac{60T_i}{n_i},\\qquad\n d(p,H)\\le1.85d_p^*+\\frac{60T_i}{n_i}\\quad(p\\in C_i\\setminus N_i).\n \\label{sam:far-client-bound}$$",

      },

Then $$|N_i(a)|\\ge(1-1/a)n_i,\\qquad\n d(i,H)\\le a\\bar d_i+\n              \\frac{T_i(a)}{(1-1/a)n_i}.\n \\label{sam:general-near-bound}$$ For the main cutoff $N_i=N_i(1.2)$ and $T_i=\\sum_{p\\in N_i}d(p,H)$ one also has $$d(i,H)\\le1.02\\bar d_i+\\frac{60T_i}{n_i},\\qquad\n d(p,H)\\le1.85d_p^*+\\frac{60T_i}{n_i}\\quad(p\\in C_i\\setminus N_i).\n \\label{sam:far-client-bound}$$",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sam-sampling-mass",
        "exact_quote": "At every correct main state with $\\kappa=10^{-6}$, $$W(H)\\le3.42P+61T_{\\rm near}(H).\n \\label{sam:weight-bound}$$ If the first stopping inequality fails, the joint event of sampling a client in $\\bigcup_{i\\in B}N_i$ and choosing ball mode has probability at least $$\\rho_\\kappa=\\frac{\\kappa}{2(3.42+61\\kappa)}>0.\n \\label{sam:progress-constant}$$ If the first stopping inequality holds and the second fails, the same lower bound holds for sampling a client assigned to $R^*$ and choosing removal mode.",

        {

          "source_id": "PM-125.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "At every correct main state with $\\kappa=10^{-6}$, $$W(H)\\le3.42P+61T_{\\rm near}(H).\n \\label{sam:weight-bound}$$ If the first stopping inequality fails, the joint event of sampling a client in $\\bigcup_{i\\in B}N_i$ and choosing ball mode has probability at least $$\\rho_\\kappa=\\frac{\\kappa}{2(3.42+61\\kappa)}>0.\n \\label{sam:progress-constant}$$ If the first stopping inequality holds and the second fails, the same lower bound holds for sampling a client assigned to $R^*$ and choosing removal mode.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sam-successful-prefix",
        "exact_quote": "Assume the equal-size comparison setup and Equation <a href=\"#sam:proxy-hypothesis\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"sam:proxy-hypothesis\">[sam:proxy-hypothesis]</a>, with $1\\le m\\le M_0$, and use $\\kappa=10^{-6}$. Given $S$ and the correct guesses $P,m$, one run of the actual experiment has, with probability at least $\\exp(-C_\\kappa m)$ for an absolute constant $C_\\kappa<\\infty$, a recorded prefix with the following properties:*\n\n1.  *$Q\\subseteq S_0$, and the balls correspond to distinct $i\\in A\\subseteq\\mathcal B$, one per such cluster, with $w_i\\in N_i$ and $$r_i=d(w_i,i)=d(w_i,O)\\le R_i\n           \\le(1+\\kappa)r_i+\\kappa r^0(w_i).$$ Consequently $i\\in\\mathcal F_i$ and $\\sum_{i\\in A}n_i r^0(w_i)\\le5.2P$.*\n\n2.  *For $H$ in Equation <a href=\"#sam:reference\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"sam:reference\">[sam:reference]</a>, $B=\\mathcal B\\setminus A$, $R^*=S_0\\setminus Q$, and the nearest assignment sets $J_s$, $$\\sum_{i\\in B}\\sum_{p\\in N_i}d(p,H)\\le\\kappa P,\n           \\qquad\n           \\sum_{s\\in R^*}\\sum_{p\\in J_s}d(p,H)\\le\\kappa P.$$*\n\n3.  *The reference retains every good proxy. The covered-client bound in Equation <a href=\"#sam:covered-client-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"sam:covered-client-bound\">[sam:covered-client-bound]</a> holds, and for each uncovered nonempty cluster the far-client bound in Equation <a href=\"#sam:far-client-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"sam:far-client-bound\">[sam:far-client-bound]</a> holds with $T_i=\\sum_{p\\in N_i}d(p,H)$.*\n\n*The run has at most $2m+1$ recorded prefixes and takes polynomial time for fixed $\\kappa$, with rational numbers of polynomial bit length. Neither its input nor its stopping decisions require $O$, its partition, the proxies, or $S_0$.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-comp-partition-max",
        "exact_quote": "Let $g$ be a normalized, monotone, submodular function given by an exact value oracle, and let $E$ have a partition into $a$ parts. For every $0<\\varepsilon<1-1/e$, a randomized algorithm uses $\\operatorname{poly}(|E|,a,1/\\varepsilon)$ oracle calls and returns a feasible $X$ satisfying $$\\mathbb E[g(X)]\\ge(1-1/e-\\varepsilon)\n       \\max\\{g(Y): |Y\\cap E_j|\\le1\\text{ for all }j\\}.$$ Every random draw can be made using a bounded polynomial number of fair bits. If the parts are nonempty, filling unoccupied parts preserves the guarantee. In particular, one obtains the factor $0.62$; more generally, for every fixed rational $\\theta>1/e$ with $\\theta<1$, one obtains expected gain at least $1-\\theta$ times the maximum.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

If the parts are nonempty, filling unoccupied parts preserves the guarantee. In particular, one obtains the factor $0.62$; more generally, for every fixed rational $\\theta>1/e$ with $\\theta<1$, one obtains expected gain at least $1-\\theta$ times the maximum.",
          "source_id": "PM-125.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-comp-completion",
        "exact_quote": "In the prefix setting above, fix a rational $1/e<\\theta<1$. Define $$R^*=S_0\\setminus Q,\\qquad\n J=\\bigcup_{s\\in R^*}J_s,\\qquad\n I=\\sum_{p\\in J}d(p,H),\\qquad\n X^*=\\{(i,i):i\\in A\\}.$$ For $p\\in C_i$, let $$c_p=\\begin{cases}\n d(p,\\phi_i),&i\\text{ good},\\\\\n d_p^*+\\theta(r_i+R_i),&i\\in A,\\\\\n d_p^*,&i\\in B,\n \\end{cases}\n \\qquad\n b_p=\\theta d(p,H)+(1-\\theta)\\min\\{d(p,H),d(p,X^*)\\}.\n \\label{comp:client-charges}$$ There is an algorithm, using only $S,m,Q$ and the given balls, whose outputs always have at most $h$ real facilities and whose work is $\\exp(O(m))\\operatorname{poly}(N,1/(\\theta-1/e))$, with polynomial dependence also on the input bit length. There is an event in its color and label choices of probability at least $\\exp(-O(m))$ such that, for every realization of those choices in the event, its output $U$ satisfies $$\\mathbb E[\\operatorname{cost}(U)]\n       \\le 2I+\\sum_{p\\in J}c_p+\\sum_{p\\notin J}b_p.\n \\label{comp:completion-bound}$$ The expectation is over the remaining optimization randomness. For good clients $b_p\\le c_p$; for clients of $A$ also $b_p\\le c_p$; for clients of $B$ one always has $b_p\\le d(p,H)$. No upper bound on $\\operatorname{cost}(H)$, proxy error, or ball radii is needed for this theorem beyond containment of $i$ in its designated ball.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

There is an event in its color and label choices of probability at least $\\exp(-O(m))$ such that, for every realization of those choices in the event, its output $U$ satisfies $$\\mathbb E[\\operatorname{cost}(U)]\n       \\le 2I+\\sum_{p\\in J}c_p+\\sum_{p\\notin J}b_p.\n \\label{comp:completion-bound}$$ The expectation is over the remaining optimization randomness. For good clients $b_p\\le c_p$; for clients of $A$ also $b_p\\le c_p$; for clients of $B$ one always has $b_p\\le d(p,H)$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-comp-recovery",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose $|S|=|O|=h\\ge1$, $P=\\operatorname{cost}(O)>0$, and $O$ has an injective proxy map into $S$ for its good centers such that $$\\sum_{i\\text{ good}}\\sum_{p\\in C_i}d(p,\\phi_i)\n       \\le P_g+0.01P.$$ Assume the number $m$ of bad centers is at most a supplied bound $M_0$. On the positive bounded integral instances used in the body of the proof, for every $0<\\zeta<1$ an algorithm knowing only $S,M_0,\\zeta$ returns a real solution of size at most $h$ and cost at most $1.97P$ with probability at least $1-\\zeta$. Its running time is $$\\exp(O(M_0))\\operatorname{poly}(N)\\log(2/\\zeta).$$ In particular this is polynomial time for every fixed constant in $M_0=O(\\log N)$. Local optimality of $S$ is not an additional hypothesis of this theorem.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-comp-recovery",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose $|S|=|O|=h\\ge1$, $P=\\operatorname{cost}(O)>0$, and $O$ has an injective proxy map into $S$ for its good centers such that $$\\sum_{i\\text{ good}}\\sum_{p\\in C_i}d(p,\\phi_i)\n       \\le P_g+0.01P.$$ Assume the number $m$ of bad centers is at most a supplied bound $M_0$. On the positive bounded integral instances used in the body of the proof, for every $0<\\zeta<1$ an algorithm knowing only $S,M_0,\\zeta$ returns a real solution of size at most $h$ and cost at most $1.97P$ with probability at least $1-\\zeta$. Its running time is $$\\exp(O(M_0))\\operatorname{poly}(N)\\log(2/\\zeta).$$ In particular this is polynomial time for every fixed constant in $M_0=O(\\log N)$. Local optimality of $S$ is not an additional hypothesis of this theorem.",
          "source_id": "PM-125.1"
        }

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ref-two-stage",
        "exact_quote": "The blind sampling procedure, run for at most $2m$ steps and retaining all prefixes, has probability at least $\\exp(-O_\\mu(m))$ of producing a prefix with the following properties.*\n\n\n\n\n*There is an earlier prefix $H^0$, with covered set $A^1$ and remaining bad set $B^0$, at which the deleted set is the final set $Q$. Put $$R^*=S_0\\setminus Q,\\qquad\n J^0=\\bigcup_{s\\in R^*}J_s(H^0),\\qquad\n T_i=\\sum_{p\\in N_i^1}d(p,H^0)\\quad(i\\in B^0).$$ Then $$\\label{ref:first-stop}\n \\sum_{i\\in B^0}T_i\\le\\kappa P,\n \\qquad \\sum_{p\\in J^0}d(p,H^0)\\le\\kappa P.$$ For each nonempty cluster $i\\in B^0$, define $f_i=|C_i\\cap J^0|/n_i$. All balls added after $H^0$ correspond to a set $A^2\\subseteq\\{i\\in B^0:n_i>0,\\ f_i<1/3\\}$, and their leaders lie in $N_i^2$; the leaders for $A^1$ lie in $N_i^1$. At the final prefix, set $A=A^1\\cup A^2$, $B=B^0\\setminus A^2$, and $J=\\bigcup_{s\\in R^*}J_s(H)$. We have $$\\label{ref:second-stop}\n J\\subseteq J^0,\\qquad\n \\sum_{p\\in J}d(p,H)\\le\\kappa P,\\qquad\n \\sum_{\\substack{i\\in B:n_i>0\\\\ f_i<1/3}}\n       \\sum_{p\\in N_i^2}d(p,H)\\le\\kappa P.$$ Every ball satisfies Equation <a href=\"#ref:radius\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ref:radius\">[ref:radius]</a>, and the collection satisfies $$\\label{ref:radius-sum}\n \\sum_{i\\in A}n_i r^0(w_i)\\le\\frac{11}{2}P.$$ The earlier prefix and the conditions defining it need not be recognized by the algorithm.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-ref-two-stage",
          "exact_quote": "The blind sampling procedure, run for at most $2m$ steps and retaining all prefixes, has probability at least $\\exp(-O_\\mu(m))$ of producing a prefix with the following properties.*\n\n\n\n\n*There is an earlier prefix $H^0$, with covered set $A^1$ and remaining bad set $B^0$, at which the deleted set is the final set $Q$. Put $$R^*=S_0\\setminus Q,\\qquad\n J^0=\\bigcup_{s\\in R^*}J_s(H^0),\\qquad\n T_i=\\sum_{p\\in N_i^1}d(p,H^0)\\quad(i\\in B^0).$$ Then $$\\label{ref:first-stop}\n \\sum_{i\\in B^0}T_i\\le\\kappa P,\n \\qquad \\sum_{p\\in J^0}d(p,H^0)\\le\\kappa P.$$ For each nonempty cluster $i\\in B^0$, define $f_i=|C_i\\cap J^0|/n_i$. All balls added after $H^0$ correspond to a set $A^2\\subseteq\\{i\\in B^0:n_i>0,\\ f_i<1/3\\}$, and their leaders lie in $N_i^2$; the leaders for $A^1$ lie in $N_i^1$. At the final prefix, set $A=A^1\\cup A^2$, $B=B^0\\setminus A^2$, and $J=\\bigcup_{s\\in R^*}J_s(H)$. We have $$\\label{ref:second-stop}\n J\\subseteq J^0,\\qquad\n \\sum_{p\\in J}d(p,H)\\le\\kappa P,\\qquad\n \\sum_{\\substack{i\\in B:n_i>0\\\\ f_i<1/3}}\n       \\sum_{p\\in N_i^2}d(p,H)\\le\\kappa P.$$ Every ball satisfies Equation <a href=\"#ref:radius\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ref:radius\">[ref:radius]</a>, and the collection satisfies $$\\label{ref:radius-sum}\n \\sum_{i\\in A}n_i r^0(w_i)\\le\\frac{11}{2}P.$$ The earlier prefix and the conditions defining it need not be recognized by the algorithm.",
          "source_id": "PM-125.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "The blind sampling procedure, run for at most $2m$ steps and retaining all prefixes, has probability at least $\\exp(-O_\\mu(m))$ of producing a prefix with the following properties.*\n\n\n\n\n*There is an earlier prefix $H^0$, with covered set $A^1$ and remaining bad set $B^0$, at which the deleted set is the final set $Q$. All balls added after $H^0$ correspond to a set $A^2\\subseteq\\{i\\in B^0:n_i>0,\\ f_i<1/3\\}$, and their leaders lie in $N_i^2$; the leaders for $A^1$ lie in $N_i^1$. At the final prefix, set $A=A^1\\cup A^2$, $B=B^0\\setminus A^2$, and $J=\\bigcup_{s\\in R^*}J_s(H)$. We have $$\\label{ref:second-stop}\n J\\subseteq J^0,\\qquad\n \\sum_{p\\in J}d(p,H)\\le\\kappa P,\\qquad\n \\sum_{\\substack{i\\in B:n_i>0\\\\ f_i<1/3}}\n       \\sum_{p\\in N_i^2}d(p,H)\\le\\kappa P.$$ Every ball satisfies Equation <a href=\"#ref:radius\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ref:radius\">[ref:radius]</a>, and the collection satisfies $$\\label{ref:radius-sum}\n \\sum_{i\\in A}n_i r^0(w_i)\\le\\frac{11}{2}P.$$ The earlier prefix and the conditions defining it need not be recognized by the algorithm.",
      "explanation": "A staged analytical guide first controls removal assignments and primary near-client cost, then adds balls only to eligible remaining clusters. Persistent leaves and fixed ties preserve the required nested assignment sets.",

      "hypotheses": [

          "anchor": "ref:parameters",
          "exact_quote": "We use the notation $P_i=\\sum_{p\\in C_i}d_p^*$, and put $\\bar d_i=P_i/n_i$ when $n_i>0$. Empty clusters have mean zero and all the near sets below are empty. Fix $$\\label{ref:parameters}\n a_1=1+\\mu,\\qquad a_2=\\frac32,\\qquad\n q=1-\\frac1{a_1}=\\frac{\\mu}{1+\\mu},\\qquad\n \\kappa=\\mu^3,\n \\qquad \\frac1e<\\theta\\le\\frac1e+\\mu,$$ where $\\theta$ is rational. For $z\\in\\{1,2\\}$ define $$N_i^z=\\{p\\in C_i:d_p^*\\le a_z\\bar d_i\\}.$$ All these parameters are fixed before the input is processed. The choice of $\\theta$ is available in Lemma <a href=\"#comp:partition-max\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"comp:partition-max\">14</a>; the resulting completion guarantee is Theorem <a href=\"#comp:completion\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"comp:completion\">16</a>.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-imp-cglss",
        "exact_quote": "For the metric range above and fixed $v$, the construction underlying Theorem 1.2 of (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson 2026) [[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)] can be run in polynomial time at any target $2\\le h\\le |F|$. With precision $\\eta=2^{-N}$, its output has one of the following forms.*\n\n1.  *A solution of $\\eta$-valid sequences with exactly $h$ distinct regular facilities and at most $L=O_v(\\log N)$ free copies, for some final price $f>0$ and specified final copy lengths.*\n\n2.  *A solution of $0$-valid sequences containing only regular facilities, at price $f=N^{-2}$, with at most $h$ facilities.*\n\n*One common integer $L$ can be used for all targets on an instance. For every regular opening, the algorithm also returns its witness set and certified bids.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-imp-cglss",

With precision $\\eta=2^{-N}$, its output has one of the following forms.*\n\n1.  *A solution of $\\eta$-valid sequences with exactly $h$ distinct regular facilities and at most $L=O_v(\\log N)$ free copies, for some final price $f>0$ and specified final copy lengths.*\n\n2.  *A solution of $0$-valid sequences containing only regular facilities, at price $f=N^{-2}$, with at most $h$ facilities.*\n\n*One common integer $L$ can be used for all targets on an instance. For every regular opening, the algorithm also returns its witness set and certified bids.",
          "source_id": "PM-125.1"
        }
      ],

With precision $\\eta=2^{-N}$, its output has one of the following forms.*\n\n1.  *A solution of $\\eta$-valid sequences with exactly $h$ distinct regular facilities and at most $L=O_v(\\log N)$ free copies, for some final price $f>0$ and specified final copy lengths.*\n\n2.  *A solution of $0$-valid sequences containing only regular facilities, at price $f=N^{-2}$, with at most $h$ facilities.*\n\n*One common integer $L$ can be used for all targets on an instance.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-imp-surplus-history",
        "exact_quote": "Given valid sequences of either output form in Theorem <a href=\"#imp:cglss\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"imp:cglss\">20</a>, with their price, fixed copy lengths, opening witnesses and certified bids, a polynomial-time replay produces final budgets $(\\alpha_p)_{p\\in D}$ and a real solution $U\\subseteq F$ of size at most $h+L$ with the following properties.*\n\n\n\n\n\n\n*The history replays the *final* sequences using their fixed final price and fixed final copy lengths. Each atomic step opens one facility or performs one simultaneous phase update. Its actual pre-step and post-step open sets satisfy $Q_t^-\\subseteq Q_t^+$, and these sets accumulate throughout the history. We call a client’s transition to inactivity its removal from the active set. Let $t(p)$ be the step at which $p$ becomes inactive and $z_p$ the phase value immediately *before* that step. At this pre-step state all active clients have budget $z_p$, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n z_p&\\le(1-\\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^-),\\label{imp:active-threshold}\\\\\n \\alpha_p&\\le Kz_p,\\label{imp:final-cap}\\\\\n (1-\\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^+)&\\le\\alpha_p\\le Kz_p.\n \\label{imp:post-removal}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The distance on the left of <a href=\"#imp:post-removal\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"imp:post-removal\">[imp:post-removal]</a> cannot increase at any later step. If $I_t$ is the actual inactive set just before step $t=t(p)$, then, for every regular facility $i\\in F$, $$\\sum_{j\\notin I_t}[z_p-d(i,j)]^+\n +\\sum_{j\\in I_t}[(1-\\delta)d(j,Q_t^-)-d(i,j)]^+\n \\le\\lambda.\n \\label{imp:no-overbid}$$ For these same final budgets, the dual inequality is $$\\sum_{p\\in D}[\\alpha_p-2d(i,p)]^+\\le\\lambda\n \\qquad(i\\in F).\n \\label{imp:dual}$$*\n\n\n\n\n*In the normal branch the history opens exactly $h$ paid regular facilities before projecting free copies, and $$(1-\\delta)\\operatorname{cost}(U)+h(\\lambda-N\\eta)\n       \\le\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p,\n \\qquad \\eta=2^{-N}.\n \\label{imp:payment}$$ In the small-price branch, $|U|\\le h$, $\\lambda=2/N^2$, $\\eta=0$, and, writing $r=|U|$, the stronger exact-payment inequality is $$(1-\\delta)\\operatorname{cost}(U)+r\\lambda\n       \\le\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p.\n \\label{imp:small-payment}$$ In particular its paid term can be dropped. For every nonempty real comparison solution $H\\subseteq F$ one also has $$\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p\n       \\le |H|\\lambda+2\\operatorname{cost}(H).\n \\label{imp:comparison}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-imp-surplus-history",
          "exact_quote": "Given valid sequences of either output form in Theorem <a href=\"#imp:cglss\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"imp:cglss\">20</a>, with their price, fixed copy lengths, opening witnesses and certified bids, a polynomial-time replay produces final budgets $(\\alpha_p)_{p\\in D}$ and a real solution $U\\subseteq F$ of size at most $h+L$ with the following properties.*\n\n\n\n\n\n\n*The history replays the *final* sequences using their fixed final price and fixed final copy lengths. Each atomic step opens one facility or performs one simultaneous phase update. Its actual pre-step and post-step open sets satisfy $Q_t^-\\subseteq Q_t^+$, and these sets accumulate throughout the history. We call a client’s transition to inactivity its removal from the active set. Let $t(p)$ be the step at which $p$ becomes inactive and $z_p$ the phase value immediately *before* that step. At this pre-step state all active clients have budget $z_p$, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n z_p&\\le(1-\\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^-),\\label{imp:active-threshold}\\\\\n \\alpha_p&\\le Kz_p,\\label{imp:final-cap}\\\\\n (1-\\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^+)&\\le\\alpha_p\\le Kz_p.\n \\label{imp:post-removal}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The distance on the left of <a href=\"#imp:post-removal\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"imp:post-removal\">[imp:post-removal]</a> cannot increase at any later step. If $I_t$ is the actual inactive set just before step $t=t(p)$, then, for every regular facility $i\\in F$, $$\\sum_{j\\notin I_t}[z_p-d(i,j)]^+\n +\\sum_{j\\in I_t}[(1-\\delta)d(j,Q_t^-)-d(i,j)]^+\n \\le\\lambda.\n \\label{imp:no-overbid}$$ For these same final budgets, the dual inequality is $$\\sum_{p\\in D}[\\alpha_p-2d(i,p)]^+\\le\\lambda\n \\qquad(i\\in F).\n \\label{imp:dual}$$*\n\n\n\n\n*In the normal branch the history opens exactly $h$ paid regular facilities before projecting free copies, and $$(1-\\delta)\\operatorname{cost}(U)+h(\\lambda-N\\eta)\n       \\le\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p,\n \\qquad \\eta=2^{-N}.\n \\label{imp:payment}$$ In the small-price branch, $|U|\\le h$, $\\lambda=2/N^2$, $\\eta=0$, and, writing $r=|U|$, the stronger exact-payment inequality is $$(1-\\delta)\\operatorname{cost}(U)+r\\lambda\n       \\le\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p.\n \\label{imp:small-payment}$$ In particular its paid term can be dropped. For every nonempty real comparison solution $H\\subseteq F$ one also has $$\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p\n       \\le |H|\\lambda+2\\operatorname{cost}(H).\n \\label{imp:comparison}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-125.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "Given valid sequences of either output form in Theorem <a href=\"#imp:cglss\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"imp:cglss\">20</a>, with their price, fixed copy lengths, opening witnesses and certified bids, a polynomial-time replay produces final budgets $(\\alpha_p)_{p\\in D}$ and a real solution $U\\subseteq F$ of size at most $h+L$ with the following properties.*\n\n\n\n\n\n\n*The history replays the *final* sequences using their fixed final price and fixed final copy lengths. Each atomic step opens one facility or performs one simultaneous phase update. Its actual pre-step and post-step open sets satisfy $Q_t^-\\subseteq Q_t^+$, and these sets accumulate throughout the history. We call a client’s transition to inactivity its removal from the active set. Let $t(p)$ be the step at which $p$ becomes inactive and $z_p$ the phase value immediately *before* that step. At this pre-step state all active clients have budget $z_p$, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n z_p&\\le(1-\\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^-),\\label{imp:active-threshold}\\\\\n \\alpha_p&\\le Kz_p,\\label{imp:final-cap}\\\\\n (1-\\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^+)&\\le\\alpha_p\\le Kz_p.\n \\label{imp:post-removal}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The distance on the left of <a href=\"#imp:post-removal\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"imp:post-removal\">[imp:post-removal]</a> cannot increase at any later step. If $I_t$ is the actual inactive set just before step $t=t(p)$, then, for every regular facility $i\\in F$, $$\\sum_{j\\notin I_t}[z_p-d(i,j)]^+\n +\\sum_{j\\in I_t}[(1-\\delta)d(j,Q_t^-)-d(i,j)]^+\n \\le\\lambda.\n \\label{imp:no-overbid}$$ For these same final budgets, the dual inequality is $$\\sum_{p\\in D}[\\alpha_p-2d(i,p)]^+\\le\\lambda\n \\qquad(i\\in F).\n \\label{imp:dual}$$*\n\n\n\n\n*In the normal branch the history opens exactly $h$ paid regular facilities before projecting free copies, and $$(1-\\delta)\\operatorname{cost}(U)+h(\\lambda-N\\eta)\n       \\le\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p,\n \\qquad \\eta=2^{-N}.\n \\label{imp:payment}$$ In the small-price branch, $|U|\\le h$, $\\lambda=2/N^2$, $\\eta=0$, and, writing $r=|U|$, the stronger exact-payment inequality is $$(1-\\delta)\\operatorname{cost}(U)+r\\lambda\n       \\le\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p.\n \\label{imp:small-payment}$$ In particular its paid term can be dropped. For every nonempty real comparison solution $H\\subseteq F$ one also has $$\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p\n       \\le |H|\\lambda+2\\operatorname{cost}(H).\n \\label{imp:comparison}$$",
      "explanation": "Replaying the final sequences at their fixed price and copy lengths produces compatible budgets for payment, no-overbidding, and final dual bounds. Projection of free copies gives a real solution with bounded surplus.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-str-strictness",
        "exact_quote": "For $T>0$ define $$\\label{str:constants}\n B=T+2,\\qquad \\rho=\\frac{1}{200B},\\qquad\n c(T)=\\frac{\\rho^2}{100}>0.$$ Assume $0\\le\\delta<1$, $0<v\\le1$, and $$\\label{str:v-condition}\n v^2\\le\\frac{c(T)}{6(T+2)}.$$ For any regular facility $i\\in F$ and nonempty client set $C\\subseteq D$ of positive cost $P_C=\\sum_{p\\in C}d(i,p)$, the history in Lemma <a href=\"#imp:surplus-history\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"imp:surplus-history\">21</a> satisfies $$\\label{str:conclusion}\n \\lambda\\le TP_C\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\sum_{p\\in C}\\alpha_p\n \\le \\lambda+\\left(2-\\frac{c(T)}2\\right)P_C.$$ The set $C$ need not be a cluster of the algorithm or of an optimal solution.",
        "source_id": "PM-125.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-str-strictness",
          "exact_quote": "For $T>0$ define $$\\label{str:constants}\n B=T+2,\\qquad \\rho=\\frac{1}{200B},\\qquad\n c(T)=\\frac{\\rho^2}{100}>0.$$ Assume $0\\le\\delta<1$, $0<v\\le1$, and $$\\label{str:v-condition}\n v^2\\le\\frac{c(T)}{6(T+2)}.$$ For any regular facility $i\\in F$ and nonempty client set $C\\subseteq D$ of positive cost $P_C=\\sum_{p\\in C}d(i,p)$, the history in Lemma <a href=\"#imp:surplus-history\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"imp:surplus-history\">21</a> satisfies $$\\label{str:conclusion}\n \\lambda\\le TP_C\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\sum_{p\\in C}\\alpha_p\n \\le \\lambda+\\left(2-\\frac{c(T)}2\\right)P_C.$$ The set $C$ need not be a cluster of the algorithm or of an optimal solution.",
          "source_id": "PM-125.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For $T>0$ define $$\\label{str:constants}\n B=T+2,\\qquad \\rho=\\frac{1}{200B},\\qquad\n c(T)=\\frac{\\rho^2}{100}>0.$$ Assume $0\\le\\delta<1$, $0<v\\le1$, and $$\\label{str:v-condition}\n v^2\\le\\frac{c(T)}{6(T+2)}.$$ For any regular facility $i\\in F$ and nonempty client set $C\\subseteq D$ of positive cost $P_C=\\sum_{p\\in C}d(i,p)$, the history in Lemma <a href=\"#imp:surplus-history\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"imp:surplus-history\">21</a> satisfies $$\\label{str:conclusion}\n \\lambda\\le TP_C\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\sum_{p\\in C}\\alpha_p\n \\le \\lambda+\\left(2-\\frac{c(T)}2\\right)P_C.$$ The set $C$ need not be a cluster of the algorithm or of an optimal solution.",
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Metric median objective and optimum** ([source](#main:introduction)): For a nonempty $S\subseteq F$ set $$\operatorname{cost}(S)=\sum_{p\in D}d(p,S),\qquad d(p,S)=\min_{s\in S}d(p,s), \qquad \operatorname{opt}_k=\min_{1\le\|S\|\le k}\operatorname{cost}(S).$$
- **Anchor** ([source](#pm-h-3)): An *anchor* is a supplied feasible set $S\subseteq F$ of the same size $h$.
- **Distinct good-center proxies** ([source](#pm-h-3)): For each good center $i$, a *proxy* is a facility $\phi_i\in S$, with the proxies pairwise distinct.
- **Comparison cluster statistics** ([source](#pre:section)): For a comparison solution $O$ of size $h$, its cluster at $i\in O$ is denoted $C_i$, and $$n_i=\|C_i\|,\qquad d_p^*=d(p,O),\qquad P_i=\sum_{p\in C_i}d_p^*,\qquad P=\operatorname{cost}(O).$$
- **Width-limited local optimum** ([source](#pm-h-9)): A set $S\subseteq F$ of size $r$ is a *width-$5$ local optimum* if no exchange removing at most five facilities and adding at most five facilities produces a set of size $r$ with strictly smaller cost.
- **Population radius** ([source](#sam:r0-definition)): Define $$r^0(w)=\min\{q\ge0:q\|\mathcal D(w,q)\|\ge P/m\}. \label{sam:r0-definition}$$
- **Reference set and facility ball** ([source](#sam:reference)): Write $\Lambda=\{\delta_i:i\in A\}$ and $$H=(S\setminus Q)\cup\Lambda\cup\{\delta_\infty\},\qquad \mathcal F_i=\{x\in F:d(w_i,x)\le R_i\}. \label{sam:reference}$$
- **Normalized monotone submodular function** ([source](#pm-h-21)): For a finite set $E$, a function $g:2^E\to\mathbb R_{\ge0}$ is *normalized* if $g(\varnothing)=0$, *monotone* if $g(U)\le g(V)$ whenever $U\subseteq V$, and *submodular* if $$g(U\cup\{e\})-g(U)\ge g(V\cup\{e\})-g(V) \qquad(U\subseteq V,\ e\notin V).$$
- **Partition constraint** ([source](#pm-h-21)): A partition constraint permits at most one element from each part of a specified partition $E=E_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup E_a$.
- **Completion surrogate** ([source](#comp:surrogate)): For a feasible $X\subseteq E$, put $$Z=\{s:s\text{ green}\}\cup\Lambda\cup\{\delta_\infty\}$$ and define the finite surrogate $$\Psi(X)=K+\sum_{p\in D}\min\{d(p,H),d(p,X)\} +\operatorname{small}_{b_{\mathrm{red}}} \{w'_s d(s,Z\cup X):s\text{ red}\}. \label{comp:surrogate}$$
- **Free copy** ([source](#pm-h-36)): A free copy $\widetilde i$ of a regular facility $i\in F$ has a specified length $u(\widetilde i)\ge0$.
- **Valid phase sequence** ([source](#pm-h-36)): A phase sequence $H=(a_1,\ldots,a_s)$ is $\eta$-valid if every entry is either a free copy or a regular facility $\eta$-openable against some witness $$W_t\supseteq Q\cup\{a_1,\ldots,a_{t-1}\},$$ and no unopened regular facility is $0$-openable against the actual set $Q\cup\{a_1,\ldots,a_s\}$ after the sequence.
- **Suffix maximum** ([source](#str:suffix)): Consequently, with $$A_p=\max_{s\ge p}(a_s-d_s),$$

| q-rounded-distance-cap | Maximum rounded distance | ≤ | $\lceil20n^2/\varepsilon_0\rceil+1$ | The rounding accuracy is fixed. |
| q-local-fallback-factor | Local solution cost | ≤ | $(17/5)P$ | Comparison cardinality does not exceed anchor cardinality. |
| q-anchoring-count-constant | Anchoring exceptional-count constant | = | $10+\frac{600}{\mu}\,2^{J_\mu-1}$ | The constant depends only on proxy accuracy. |
| q-main-anchoring-constant | Main exceptional-count constant | = | $10+60000\cdot2^{3999}$ | Fixed before surplus parameters are chosen. |
| q-amortized-menu-indices | Sum of prescribed third-menu indices | ≤ | $\left(\frac{10}{9\kappa}+1\right)m$ | At most the allowed number of ball additions. |
| q-main-sampling-weight | Total reference sampling weight | ≤ | $3.42P+61T_{\rm near}(H)$ | The sampling resolution is fixed. |

| q-recovery-cost-factor | Recovered solution cost | ≤ | $1.97P$ | Successful event of the amplified recovery algorithm. |
| q-refined-charge-factor | Refined completion charge bound | ≤ | $(1+2/e+6\mu)P$ | Conditional on successful completion colors and labels. |

| q-global-improvement-constant | Global approximation improvement | = | $\gamma/4$ | An absolute fixed constant. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-rounding** [claim](#stmt-pre-rounding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-rounding): Detect zero optimum, enumerate a largest optimum assignment distance, round and cap distances, then select by original cost. -> Budget-preserving transfer with controlled rounding loss.
- **step-local-descent** [claim](#stmt-loc-descent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-descent): Enumerate bounded exchanges and perform strict integral improvements. -> A local optimum without increased cost.
- **step-weighted-swaps** [claim](#stmt-loc-swap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-swap): Protect proxies, allocate unprotected slots, and sum weighted exchange-routing inequalities. -> Weighted cost inequality and unconditional fallback.
- **step-anchoring** [claim](#stmt-loc-anchoring) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-anchoring): Average geometric thresholds, separate proxy balls, and charge crowded, outgoing, and incoming clients. -> Existential good proxies and bounded bad-center count.
- **step-population-radius** [claim](#stmt-sam-computable-radius) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sam-computable-radius): Minimize over sorted client distances and certify feasibility by ball population. -> Computable attained radius and aggregate leader bound.
- **step-reference-extension** [claim](#stmt-sam-dummy-metric) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sam-dummy-metric): Attach leaves in a shortest-path metric and append new leaves to a fixed assignment order. -> Metric domination and assignment monotonicity.
- **step-menu-amortization** [claim](#stmt-sam-radius-budget) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sam-radius-budget): Contradict overlap using earlier leaves, then charge disjoint client populations to comparison cost. -> Aggregate menu-index and likelihood bounds.
- **step-guided-likelihood** [claim](#stmt-sam-probability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sam-probability): Compare conditional transition probabilities and sum disjoint terminal cylinders. -> Single-exponential probability of recording a guided terminal prefix.
- **step-near-client-bounds** [claim](#stmt-sam-near-clients) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sam-near-clients): Use cluster averaging and the triangle inequality through a near client. -> Near population and far-client distance bounds.
- **step-progress-mass** [claim](#stmt-sam-sampling-mass) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sam-sampling-mass): Sum contributions of good, covered, and uncovered clusters. -> Positive progress probability whenever stopping fails.
- **step-successful-prefix** [claim](#stmt-sam-successful-prefix) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sam-successful-prefix): Guide each transition toward a new covered cluster or a fresh nonproxy removal. -> Recorded prefix satisfying both stopping inequalities.
- **step-partition-maximization** [claim](#stmt-comp-partition-max) / [proof](#proof-stmt-comp-partition-max): Estimate categorical derivatives, integrate feasible directions, and sample the final categorical set. -> Expected approximation with bounded fair-bit draws.
- **step-surrogate-realization** [claim](#stmt-comp-surrogate-properties) / [proof](#proof-stmt-comp-surrogate-properties): Fill balls, realize blue destinations, choose red removals, and express savings as maxima and truncated coverage. -> Real feasible solution and submodular savings.
- **step-completion** [claim](#stmt-comp-completion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-comp-completion): Use favorable colors and distinct labels, exact destination optimization, and a convex surrogate comparison. -> Conditional expected client-charge bound.
- **step-basic-recovery** [claim](#stmt-comp-recovery) / [proof](#proof-stmt-comp-recovery): Complete every recorded prefix, apply the nonnegative-cost probability bound, and repeat over all parameter guesses. -> Amplified recovery within the original facility budget.
- **step-two-stage-endpoint** [claim](#stmt-ref-two-stage) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ref-two-stage): Stop the removal stage, freeze assignment fractions, then add balls to eligible remaining clusters. -> Nested assignment sets and refined endpoint bounds.
- **step-refined-charges** [claim](#stmt-ref-charges) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ref-charges): Separate cluster classes and sum shared near-client and radius errors only once. -> Refined expected-cost coefficient.
- **step-refined-recovery** [claim](#stmt-ref-recovery) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ref-charges-2): Extract positive success probability from the refined expectation and amplify across cost and count guesses. -> Refined anchor recovery theorem.
- **step-surplus-interface** [claim](#stmt-imp-cglss) / [proof](#imp:instantiation): Instantiate the external merge construction with checked endpoints, precision, paid counts, and disabling lengths. -> Certified final valid sequences in either output form.
- **step-compatible-history** [claim](#stmt-imp-surplus-history) / [proof](#proof-stmt-imp-surplus-history): Replay fixed final sequences, maintain the payment potential, prove atomic no-overbidding and the final dual, then project copies. -> Compatible payment, comparison, and dual budgets.
- **step-bounded-price-gap** [claim](#stmt-str-strictness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-str-strictness): Derive suffix inequalities and complementary slack bounds; contradict a small deficit using early and late indices. -> Strict cluster budget bound retaining the opening payment.
- **step-low-sensitivity** [claim](#stmt-sur-low-sensitivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sur-low-sensitivity): Bound price, establish the anchoring scale, and split between small bad cost and bounded-price bad clusters. -> Feasible candidate with strict cost improvement.
- **step-sensitive-cardinality** [claim](#stmt-asm-sensitive) / [proof](#proof-stmt-asm-sensitive): Use the product of consecutive optimum ratios and compare deletion rerouting costs. -> Nearby sensitive cardinality with separated nonempty clusters.
- **step-integral-assembly** [claim](#stmt-asm-integral) / [proof](#proof-stmt-asm-integral): Retain the fallback and compute low-sensitivity and recovery candidates across possible cardinalities. -> Integral-instance approximation with bounded failure cost.
- **step-global-transfer** [claim](#stmt-main-theorem) / [proof](#proof-stmt-asm-integral-2): Apply rounding, select the least original-cost candidate, and split expectation over success and failure. -> Global probability and expectation guarantees.

Each atomic step opens one facility or performs one simultaneous phase update. Its actual pre-step and post-step open sets satisfy $Q_t^-\\subseteq Q_t^+$, and these sets accumulate throughout the history. We call a client’s transition to inactivity its removal from the active set. Let $t(p)$ be the step at which $p$ becomes inactive and $z_p$ the phase value immediately *before* that step. At this pre-step state all active clients have budget $z_p$, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n z_p&\\le(1-\\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^-),\\label{imp:active-threshold}\\\\\n \\alpha_p&\\le Kz_p,\\label{imp:final-cap}\\\\\n (1-\\delta)d(p,Q_{t(p)}^+)&\\le\\alpha_p\\le Kz_p.\n \\label{imp:post-removal}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The distance on the left of <a href=\"#imp:post-removal\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"imp:post-removal\">[imp:post-removal]</a> cannot increase at any later step. If $I_t$ is the actual inactive set just before step $t=t(p)$, then, for every regular facility $i\\in F$, $$\\sum_{j\\notin I_t}[z_p-d(i,j)]^+\n +\\sum_{j\\in I_t}[(1-\\delta)d(j,Q_t^-)-d(i,j)]^+\n \\le\\lambda.\n \\label{imp:no-overbid}$$ For these same final budgets, the dual inequality is $$\\sum_{p\\in D}[\\alpha_p-2d(i,p)]^+\\le\\lambda\n \\qquad(i\\in F).\n \\label{imp:dual}$$*\n\n\n\n\n*In the normal branch the history opens exactly $h$ paid regular facilities before projecting free copies, and $$(1-\\delta)\\operatorname{cost}(U)+h(\\lambda-N\\eta)\n       \\le\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p,\n \\qquad \\eta=2^{-N}.\n \\label{imp:payment}$$ In the small-price branch, $|U|\\le h$, $\\lambda=2/N^2$, $\\eta=0$, and, writing $r=|U|$, the stronger exact-payment inequality is $$(1-\\delta)\\operatorname{cost}(U)+r\\lambda\n       \\le\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p.\n \\label{imp:small-payment}$$ In particular its paid term can be dropped. For every nonempty real comparison solution $H\\subseteq F$ one also has $$\\sum_{p\\in D}\\alpha_p\n       \\le |H|\\lambda+2\\operatorname{cost}(H).\n \\label{imp:comparison}$$",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **invariant-population-radius**: The population radius is the minimum, over sorted client-distance prefixes, of the larger of the prefix distance and its population threshold.
- **invariant-attained-positive-radius**: The computed radius is positive, attains the minimum, and its closed client ball satisfies the defining population inequality.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **antiuse-omit-leader**: Compute the population radius from distances to other clients while omitting the leader. -> The closed-ball population is changed, so the computed value need not be the radius defined in the paper.
- **antiuse-assume-cost-guess-correct**: Treat an arbitrary guessed cost and bad-center count as the true comparison parameters. -> The radius primitive is computable for guesses, but its comparison-dependent bounds require the correct analytical parameters.
- **antiuse-promote-radius-check-to-recovery-proof**: Use successful radius computations as certification of the complete recovery approximation. -> The finite check covers the population-radius primitive and does not establish sampling likelihood, proxy accuracy, completion quality, or the recovery approximation.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The finite population-radius formula in the computable-radius lemma gives an exact constructive primitive. The executable check covers this primitive and its attained population inequality, rather than the complete randomized recovery algorithm.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON with d, a sorted list of nonnegative integer distances including zero; P, a positive rational string; m, a positive integer.
# PRECONDITIONS: The distance list has between one and eight entries; its first entry is zero; P and m are positive.
# OUTPUTS: The exact population radius as a reduced numerator/denominator string.
# TIME: Linear many exact rational comparisons after input validation; integer arithmetic depends on operand bit lengths.
# SPACE: Linear in the input representation and rational operand sizes.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact integer and rational arithmetic throughout.
# FAILURE: Invalid inputs terminate with a nonzero exit status.
import json
import sys
from fractions import Fraction

obj = json.load(sys.stdin)
d = obj['d']
m = obj['m']
P = Fraction(obj['P'])
if not isinstance(d, list) or not 1 <= len(d) <= 8:
    raise ValueError('invalid distance list')
if any(type(x) is not int or x < 0 for x in d):
    raise ValueError('invalid distance')
if d != sorted(d) or d[0] != 0:
    raise ValueError('distances must be sorted and include zero')
if type(m) is not int or m <= 0 or P <= 0:
    raise ValueError('invalid population parameters')
q = min(max(Fraction(distance), P / (m * j)) for j, distance in enumerate(d, 1))
print(str(q.numerator) + '/' + str(q.denominator))
```
