     \le(1-\delta)vz<z\le\tau_j.$$ Consequently every client remaining active has its old budget $z$. At a free opening no budgets increase and the same conclusion holds. At a phase update, with old value $z$, each active budget becomes $$\beta_j'=\min\{Kz,(1-\delta)d(j,Q)\};$$ clients reaching the second value become inactive, and all others have budget $Kz$, the next phase value. This proves inductively both the common active budget and the identity $A=A_Q$ at every atomic boundary. It gives the strict form of <a href="#imp:active-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:active-threshold">[imp:active-threshold]</a> whenever the right side is finite.

When a regular opening inactivates a client, its final bid is at most $Kz_p$ and at least its new scaled connection distance. Inactivation during a free opening has final bid exactly $z_p$. At inactivation during a phase update, the final bid equals the scaled connection distance and is at most $Kz_p$. In every case the bid is frozen after removal from the active set. This proves <a href="#imp:final-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:final-cap">[imp:final-cap]</a>–<a href="#imp:post-removal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:post-removal">[imp:post-removal]</a>, using the old phase value also for a phase-update removal. Since $Q$ only grows in the fixed metric extension, later connection distances cannot increase. Several removals in one atomic step all use the same pre-step value and pre-step set; an ordering of tied clients does not insert fictitious intermediate states.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
###### No overbidding at actual atomic boundaries.

We include the invariant argument corresponding to Claim 10, Equation (33), in Appendix A.2 of (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson 2026) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)]. At a boundary set $c_j=(1-\delta)d(j,Q)$ and define $$B_i=\sum_{j\in A}[z-d(i,j)]^+
          +\sum_{j\in I}[c_j-d(i,j)]^+.$$ Initially $B_i=0$ because all client–facility distances are at least one. An opening cannot increase any client’s term. Old inactive clients have smaller or unchanged $c_j$; an active client that stays active keeps $z$; and an active client that becomes inactive has new $c_j\le z$. The latter assertion holds even if its bid increased, because an increased bid is possible only within $vz$ of the opening facility. A free opening has the same monotonicity without bid increases. This proves preservation at opening steps, including openings witnessed by supersets.

Consider a phase update after a maximal phase sequence. For its old active set put $b_j(t)=\min\{t,c_j\}$ for $z\le t\le Kz$, and define $$B_i(t)=\sum_{j\in A}[b_j(t)-d(i,j)]^+
             +\sum_{j\in I}[c_j-d(i,j)]^+.$$ At $t=Kz$ this is precisely the invariant after the simultaneous update, including newly inactive clients. If some $B_i(Kz)>\lambda$, continuity gives a first $t_*\in[z,Kz]$ at which some such facility $i$ has $B_i(t_*)=\lambda$; choose the first crossing among all regular facilities. Then $B_{i_0}(t_*)\le\lambda$ for every $i_0\in F$. Such an $i$ is unopened, since a facility in $Q$ has $B_i(t)=0$ for all these $t$.

Offer $\tau_j=b_j(t_*)$ for $d(i,j)\le vz$ and $\tau_j=z$ otherwise. These bids satisfy the two update restrictions. For an outside client, $\delta d(i,j)\ge3v^2z\ge v^2z$, so $$z-(1-\delta)d(i,j)\ge b_j(t_*)-d(i,j).$$ For a nearby client this comparison holds immediately with $z$ replaced by $b_j(t_*)$. Also $[c_j-(1-\delta)d(i,j)]^+\ge[c_j-d(i,j)]^+$ for an inactive client. Thus these bids pay at least $B_i(t_*)=\lambda$. Finally, for every $a\in A$, $j\in I$, and $i_0\in F$, the triangle inequality and $\tau_a\le c_a$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 \tau_a-d(i_0,a)-2d(i_0,j)
 &\le (1-\delta)d(j,Q)-d(i_0,j)\\
 &=c_j-d(i_0,j).
\end{aligned}$$ Since $\tau_j\le b_j(t_*)$ for active $j$, the left side of <a href="#imp:opening-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:opening-dual">[imp:opening-dual]</a> is at most $B_{i_0}(t_*)\le\lambda$. Hence $i$ is $0$-openable against the actual set $Q$, contradicting maximality. This proves the invariant through phase updates and establishes <a href="#imp:no-overbid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:no-overbid">[imp:no-overbid]</a> at precisely the required states.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
###### Payment with witness supersets.

The certificate’s payment can be checked directly by a potential argument; this is the accounting in Lemma A.1 and Equation (32) of (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson 2026) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)]. First compare a regular opening’s witness payments with its actual payments. For $j\in A_Q\setminus A_W$ we have $z\ge(1-\delta)d(j,W)$, whence $$[z-(1-\delta)d(i,j)]^+
 \ge[(1-\delta)d(j,W)-(1-\delta)d(i,j)]^+.$$ For $j\in I_Q$, replacing $W$ by $Q\subseteq W$ only increases the available saving. Clients in $A_W$ keep their certified bids. Therefore <a href="#imp:opening-payment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:opening-payment">[imp:opening-payment]</a> remains valid against the *actual* prefix with the extended actual bids.

<a id="imp:virtual-payment"></a>
Let $r_Q$ count regular facilities opened in the actual prefix. At every boundary consider $$\mathcal P=\sum_{j\in I}\bigl(\beta_j-(1-\delta)d(j,Q)\bigr)
              -r_Q(\lambda-n\eta).$$ Initially it is zero. At a regular opening, already inactive clients contribute their nonnegative connection savings. For an old active client a positive contribution $[\tau_j-(1-\delta)d(i,j)]^+$ makes it inactive, and its new slack $\tau_j-(1-\delta)d(j,Q\cup\{i\})$ is at least that contribution. The total increase before the paid-facility term is therefore at least $\lambda-n\eta$. At a free opening, old inactive slacks can only increase and new inactive slacks are nonnegative. At a phase update, the new inactive clients have zero slack, while the old terms and $r_Q$ are unchanged. Thus $\mathcal P\ge0$ always. At termination, writing $V$ for the final virtual facilities and $r$ for its regular count, this gives $$(1-\delta)\sum_{j\in D}d(j,V)+r(\lambda-n\eta)
           \le\sum_{j\in D}\alpha_j.
 \label{imp:virtual-payment}$$

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
###### The final dual on this replay.

We prove <a href="#imp:dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:dual">[imp:dual]</a> for the budgets just constructed. The source defines this execution in Section 3.4 and immediately before Appendix A.1. Its final-dual statement is Lemma A.2, used to prove Theorem 3.10. Our argument follows Lemmas A.2–A.3 and Equations (34)–(38) of (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson 2026, Appendix A.2) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)], whose regular-opening argument already handles witness supersets. We give the rows explicitly, including the case where the first facility that freezes the comparison facility is free. That case needs the no-overbidding invariant in place of an opening-dual condition.

Fix $i\in F$, write $d_j=d(i,j)$, and put $$D^*=\{j\in D:\alpha_j>2d_j\},\qquad m=|D^*|.$$ There is nothing to prove if $m=0$. Order $D^*$ by removal step, breaking ties by nondecreasing final budget $\alpha_j$, and then arbitrarily between equal budgets. Write $j\prec k$ and $j\succeq k$ for this order. This auxiliary order is specific to the present dual proof; it imposes no restriction on the arbitrary order of simultaneous removals allowed in the later strictness argument. In particular it does not create intermediate states within an atomic step.

<a id="imp:pre-freeze-row"></a>
<a id="imp:triangle-row"></a>
An opening of $h$ at phase value $z$ *freezes* $i$ if $d(i,h)\le(1+3v)z/2$. We use only the first such opening, if there is one. We first establish the following row for each contributor $k$ removed strictly before that opening, or for every $k\in D^*$ if no opening freezes $i$: $$\label{imp:pre-freeze-row}
 \sum_{\substack{j\in D^*\\j\succeq k}}[\alpha_k-d_j]^+
 +\sum_{\substack{j\in D^*\\j\prec k}}
       [\alpha_k-2d_j-d_k]^+\le\lambda.$$ The elementary comparison used below is that, for a nonempty actual prefix $Q$, $c_j=(1-\delta)d(j,Q)$, and $u\le c_k$, $$\label{imp:triangle-row}
 u-2d_j-d_k\le c_j-d_j.$$ Indeed, $d(k,Q)\le d_k+d_j+d(j,Q)$, and multiplication by $1-\delta\le1$ proves the assertion. When $Q=\varnothing$ there are no inactive clients, so none of the applications to inactive clients below is needed.

Suppose first that $k$ is removed by opening $h$ at phase value $z$, before $i$ is frozen. No contributor can have its budget increased by this opening: such an increase would require $d(h,j)\le vz$, whereas $$2d_j\ge2d(i,h)-2d(h,j)>(1+v)z\ge Kz\ge\alpha_j,$$ contradicting $j\in D^*$. Thus $\alpha_k=z$. Apply no overbidding at the actual boundary immediately before the opening. Every $j\succeq k$ is then active, so its term is exactly $[\alpha_k-d_j]^+$. A $j\prec k$ removed in the same opening is also still active at this boundary, and its term $[z-d_j]^+$ dominates $[\alpha_k-2d_j-d_k]^+$. Any remaining $j\prec k$ is already inactive; actual activity of $k$ gives $\alpha_k=z<c_k$, so <a href="#imp:triangle-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:triangle-row">[imp:triangle-row]</a> gives the required domination for $j$. Discarding noncontributors proves <a href="#imp:pre-freeze-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:pre-freeze-row">[imp:pre-freeze-row]</a>.

Suppose instead that a simultaneous phase update removes $k$, with old phase value $z$. Use the no-overbidding invariant at the actual *post-update* boundary. The set $Q$ is unchanged and $\alpha_k=c_k\le Kz$. Clients still active have budget $Kz$, which dominates the budget appearing in either term of <a href="#imp:pre-freeze-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:pre-freeze-row">[imp:pre-freeze-row]</a>. For an inactive $j\prec k$, use <a href="#imp:triangle-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:triangle-row">[imp:triangle-row]</a>. An inactive $j\succeq k$ must have been removed in this same update. Therefore $c_j=\alpha_j\ge\alpha_k$, where the last inequality is precisely the reason for the final-budget tie rule. Its invariant term dominates $[\alpha_k-d_j]^+$. This proves <a href="#imp:pre-freeze-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:pre-freeze-row">[imp:pre-freeze-row]</a> also for phase-update removals.

If no opening freezes $i$, sum <a href="#imp:pre-freeze-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:pre-freeze-row">[imp:pre-freeze-row]</a> over all $k\in D^*$. Replace each positive part by its argument. Each budget has coefficient $m$. If $j$ has rank $q$, its distance occurs with coefficient $q+2(m-q)+(q-1)=2m-1$. Hence $$m\sum_{j\in D^*}\alpha_j-(2m-1)\sum_{j\in D^*}d_j
       \le m\lambda,$$ which implies $\sum_{j\in D^*}(\alpha_j-2d_j)\le\lambda$.

It remains to consider a first freezing opening $h$ at phase value $z$. Let $Q,A,I$ be the actual state immediately before it, and let $b_j$ be the extended actual bid used at this opening: certified bids for witness-active clients and $z$ for the other active clients if $h$ is regular, and $b_j=z$ for all $j\in A$ if $h$ is free. Every $j\in A\cap D^*$ is removed at this opening and has $\alpha_j=b_j$. To see this, suppose that $j$ remains active. Then $z\le b_j<(1-\delta)d(j,h)$. Every later actual prefix, and thus every later witness, contains $h$. Phase updates and certified bid updates are consequently capped by $(1-\delta)d(j,h)$; unchanged active bids also satisfy this bound. It follows that $\alpha_j\le(1-\delta)d(j,h)$. But $$2d_j\ge2d(j,h)-(1+3v)z
    >\bigl(2-(1+3v)(1-\delta)\bigr)d(j,h)
     =(1+9v^2)d(j,h)>\alpha_j,$$ a contradiction. This argument applies to free and regular openings and uses only containment of the actual prefix in future witnesses.

<a id="imp:witness-freeze-row"></a>
<a id="imp:unchanged-freeze-row"></a>
Write $R=I\cap D^*$. These contributors precede the freezing opening, so they retain all rows <a href="#imp:pre-freeze-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:pre-freeze-row">[imp:pre-freeze-row]</a>. If $h$ is regular, let $W\supseteq Q$ be its witness and set $$P=A_W\cap D^*,\qquad T=(A\setminus A_W)\cap D^*.$$ For $j\in P$ the certified bid equals $\alpha_j$, whereas for $j\in T$ the unchanged actual bid gives $\alpha_j=z$. Restricting <a href="#imp:opening-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:opening-dual">[imp:opening-dual]</a> to contributors gives, for every $k\in P$, $$\label{imp:witness-freeze-row}
 \sum_{j\in P}[\alpha_j-d_j]^+
 +\sum_{j\in R\cup T}[\alpha_k-2d_j-d_k]^+\le\lambda.$$ For each $k\in T$, apply actual no overbidding immediately before the opening. The active budget is $z=\alpha_k$, and $k$ is actually active, so $\alpha_k<c_k$. Using <a href="#imp:triangle-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:triangle-row">[imp:triangle-row]</a> for inactive clients gives $$\label{imp:unchanged-freeze-row}
 \sum_{j\in P\cup T}[\alpha_k-d_j]^+
 +\sum_{j\in R}[\alpha_k-2d_j-d_k]^+\le\lambda.$$ Thus actual-active but witness-inactive contributors use the actual invariant, without assuming that the two active sets coincide.

If $h$ is free, set $P=\varnothing$ and $T=A\cap D^*$. The preceding freezing argument shows that every $k\in T$ is removed by this free opening with $\alpha_k=z$. The same actual pre-opening invariant and <a href="#imp:triangle-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:triangle-row">[imp:triangle-row]</a> prove <a href="#imp:unchanged-freeze-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:unchanged-freeze-row">[imp:unchanged-freeze-row]</a> for each such $k$. No witness or opening-dual condition is needed for the free opening. This supplies the free first-freezing case omitted by the invocation of an opening-dual condition in the cited proof.

In either case put $r=|R|$, $p=|P|$, and $t=|T|$, so $m=r+p+t$. Sum <a href="#imp:pre-freeze-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:pre-freeze-row">[imp:pre-freeze-row]</a> for $k\in R$, <a href="#imp:witness-freeze-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:witness-freeze-row">[imp:witness-freeze-row]</a> for $k\in P$, and <a href="#imp:unchanged-freeze-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:unchanged-freeze-row">[imp:unchanged-freeze-row]</a> for $k\in T$, replacing every positive part by its argument. Each budget has coefficient $m$: for $R$ and $T$ it occurs $m$ times in its own row, and for $P$ it occurs in the first sum of each of the $p$ rows indexed by $P$ and $m-p$ times in its own second sum. The distance coefficients are $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 \text{client class}&\text{coefficient calculation}&\text{coefficient}\\\hline
 j\in R\text{, rank }q\text{ in }R
    &q+2(r-q)+(q-1)+2p+2t&2m-1\\
 j\in T&r+2p+t+r&2m-t\\
 j\in P&r+t+p+(r+t)&2m-p.
 \end{array}$$ Consequently $$m\sum_{j\in D^*}\alpha_j
 -(2m-1)\sum_{j\in R}d_j
 -(2m-t)\sum_{j\in T}d_j
 -(2m-p)\sum_{j\in P}d_j\le m\lambda.$$ Every displayed distance coefficient is at most $2m$; subtracting the remaining nonnegative distance terms and dividing by $m$ proves $$\sum_{j\in D}[\alpha_j-2d(i,j)]^+
   =\sum_{j\in D^*}(\alpha_j-2d_j)\le\lambda.$$ This is <a href="#imp:dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:dual">[imp:dual]</a> for the same final budgets used in the payment argument. It also holds for each free copy by distance domination, although only $i\in F$ is needed here.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
###### Projection and cardinality.

Project each free copy of $i$ to $i$ and discard duplicate locations, giving $U\subseteq F$. For every client, $d(j,U)\le d(j,V)$, so projection can only decrease cost. In the normal branch $r=h$ before projection and $|U|\le h+L$; substituting into <a href="#imp:virtual-payment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:virtual-payment">[imp:virtual-payment]</a> and using $n\le N$ proves <a href="#imp:payment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:payment">[imp:payment]</a>. Coincident projected locations do not reduce the number $h$ of paid regular facilities in that inequality. In the unmerged branch there are no free copies, $r=|U|\le h$, and $\eta=0$, giving <a href="#imp:small-payment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:small-payment">[imp:small-payment]</a>. Finally assign every client to its nearest facility of a nonempty $H$. For each $i\in H$, the elementary bound $$\sum_{j\text{ assigned to }i}\alpha_j
 \le2\sum_{j\text{ assigned to }i}d(i,j)
       +\sum_{j\in D}[\alpha_j-2d(i,j)]^+$$ and <a href="#imp:dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:dual">[imp:dual]</a> give <a href="#imp:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="imp:comparison">[imp:comparison]</a> upon summation. All statements therefore use one and the same history and budgets. ◻

</details>

<a id="imp:instantiation"></a>
#### Instantiating the construction

We now verify the choices needed for Theorem <a href="#imp:cglss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:cglss">20</a>. The merging procedure itself is that of (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson 2026, sec. 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)]. It uses at most three free copies per phase and stops at exactly the target number of regular facilities. We check its endpoints and parameter transfers for the metric range and precision used here.

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
###### Price range and precision.

Initialize at $f=N^{-2}$ and retain the source’s upper price $4nM$, where $M$ bounds all original distances. At this upper endpoint, the first opening in an unmerged execution satisfies $nKz\ge\lambda=8nM$, since every active bid is at most $Kz$. Hence $z>M$, that opening makes all clients inactive, and the total subsequent switching payment is at most $nM<\lambda$. The upper-endpoint solution therefore has one facility, as required to bracket $h\ge2$. At the lower endpoint only cardinality is tested: if the unmerged run has at most $h$ facilities, return it as the small-price alternative. Section <a href="#sur:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sur:section">9</a> proves its cost bound using strictness.

Perform each binary search to parameter difference at most $\eta/2$, where $\eta=2^{-N}$. A price difference of at most $\eta/2$ changes $\lambda=2f$ by at most $\eta\le n\eta$; a copy-length difference of at most $\eta/2$ changes the total payment by at most $n\eta/2$. Thus the source’s parameter-transfer argument supplies the stated $\eta$-valid sequences, including when $n=1$. The source maintains a common $\eta$-valid prefix of earlier phases and transfers a current $0$-valid phase across one changed parameter (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson 2026, sec. 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)]. Thus the per-opening validity tolerance is not compounded from phase to phase.

The transfer also preserves the unrelaxed opening-dual condition. Here is the scaled comparison needed when a client becomes inactive under a changed copy length, completing the corresponding calculation in (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson 2026, sec. 3.5.1, Claim 2) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)]. Use the new metric $d$ and witness $W$, and put $t=1-\delta$. If $j$ was active and becomes witness-inactive while $\ell$ remains witness-active, the former active bid $\tau_j$, old phase value $z$, and new clipped bid $\tau'_\ell$ satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 \tau_j&\ge z\ge t\,d(j,W)\\
 &\ge t\,d(\ell,W)-t\,d(j,\ell)\\
 &\ge\tau'_\ell-d(j,i_0)-d(\ell,i_0)
       \qquad(i_0\in F).
 \end{aligned}$$ Subtracting $d(i_0,j)$ and taking positive parts shows that the former active term dominates the new inactive dual term. The scaled distance $t\,d(j,W)$, rather than $d(j,W)$, is what the transfer hypothesis supplies. Together with clipping of surviving active bids, this is the dual comparison; the payment tolerance is not added to the dual inequality.

Witnesses and bids can be retained explicitly through the merge. Deleting an earlier entry preserves the superset relation for an inherited witness; a parameter transfer clips its inherited bids at the new caps; and a newly completed entry uses its actual prefix. These are polynomial-size certificates. Fix deterministic LP and tie-breaking choices in the source’s completion subroutine when comparing two interpolation endpoints.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
###### Paid counts at interpolation endpoints.

At a zero-length-copy endpoint, a regular facility and its free copy are geometrically coincident but have different paid counts. In particular, the corresponding continuations can differ by one regular facility; the geometric endpoint identities in (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, Schwiegelshohn, and Svensson 2026, sec. 3.5.2, Case 2; Section 3.5.3, Claim 5 and Case 3) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)] must not be used as equalities of paid counts. Test each endpoint explicitly for the target count $h$. If an endpoint has count $h$, return it; otherwise a change by one cannot pass from strictly below $h$ to strictly above $h$. This gives the needed integer bracketing for the interpolation while preserving exactly $h$ paid regular facilities in the normal branch. No claim of exactly $h$ distinct projected facilities is used.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
###### Disabling copies and bounding the phase count.

Replace the fixed disabling length $10M$ used for free copies in the source’s Section 3.5 by $20NM$. This ensures that the endpoint of a copy-length search has no effect except the copy’s label. Indeed, the no-overbidding invariant in Lemma <a href="#imp:surplus-history" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:surplus-history">21</a> depends only on valid sequences. For any active client $j$ at any atomic boundary it implies $[z-d(i,j)]^+\le\lambda$ for every regular $i$, and hence $$z\le M+\lambda\le Z:=M+8NM.$$ An inactive client’s scaled connection distance is at most its frozen budget, which is at most $KZ$. Since $v<1/6$ and $N\ge3$, $$KZ\le\frac{37}{36}(M+8NM)<10NM<(1-\delta)20NM.$$ A copy of length $20NM$ therefore cannot inactivate an active client, bind any bid cap at most $Kz$, or reduce an inactive client’s connection distance. These observations apply also to witness sets: their scaled inactive connection distances are at most $z$, and their active bid caps are at most $Kz$. The disabling endpoint consequently leaves all subsequent openability tests and updates unchanged.

All these parameters have polynomial bit length. Their binary searches remain polynomial. The bound $z\le Z$ and geometric phase growth give $O_v(\log N)$ possible active phase indices, uniformly over the search range. Multiplying a common phase bound by the source’s at most three free copies per phase gives the asserted uniform $L$.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
###### Precision and parameter order.

The total payment error in the normal branch is <a id="imp:precision"></a>$$e=hN\eta\le N^2 2^{-N}.
 \label{imp:precision}$$ For every fixed $v>0$ there is a fixed $N_v$ such that $N^2 2^{-N}\le v$ for all $N\ge N_v$. Every positive integral comparison cost $P$ is at least one, so $e\le vP$ in that range. The binary representation of $\eta$ has $O(N)$ bits. Increasing the constant in $L=O_v(\log N)$ can place every $N<N_v$ in the branch $k\le2L$, where the logarithmic recovery procedure is used instead. Thus constants in the local and strictness arguments can be chosen first, then $v$, then the common surplus bound and its fixed-size enlargement. No dependence of those earlier constants on $L$ is introduced.

<a id="str:section"></a>
### Strictness at a bounded opening price

Bounded-price strictness was quantified in the factor-revealing analysis of (Cohen-Addad, Grandoni, Lee, and Schwiegelshohn 2026, Appendix B, Corollary 2) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[CGLS2026](#ref-CGLS2026)], following the JMS dual-fitting framework (Jain, Mahdian, and Saberi 2002; Jain et al. 2003) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[JMS2002](#ref-JMS2002), [JainEtAl2003](#ref-JainEtAl2003)]. That work proves the gap $1/[4(7+3T)]$ for its JMS program. Moreover, its fixed-size dual construction in Appendix B.1–B.3 applies to the relaxed scalar inequalities used below: it does not need either ordered phase variables or the first-connection constraint. It gives a stronger gap than the constant we will use. The contribution of this section is a different elementary proof, through a suffix maximum and two slack bounds, followed by an explicit transfer to the fixed valid-sequence replay. We retain a small explicit gap because that is sufficient for the payment argument.

Write $[x]_+=\max\{x,0\}$. The following argument uses the history of one fixed final valid sequence from Lemma <a href="#imp:surplus-history" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:surplus-history">21</a>. In particular, all prices, free-copy lengths, budgets, and client-removal times below belong to that one replay. Put $t=1-\delta$ and $K=1+v^2$. The opened sets in the replay increase, although the algorithm that finds the final sequence need not have this monotonicity.

<a id="stmt-str-strictness"></a>
<div id="str:strictness" class="lemma">

<a id="str:constants"></a>
<a id="str:v-condition"></a>
<a id="str:conclusion"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Bounded-price strictness). *For $T>0$ define $$\label{str:constants}
 B=T+2,\qquad \rho=\frac{1}{200B},\qquad
 c(T)=\frac{\rho^2}{100}>0.$$ Assume $0\le\delta<1$, $0<v\le1$, and $$\label{str:v-condition}
 v^2\le\frac{c(T)}{6(T+2)}.$$ For any regular facility $i\in F$ and nonempty client set $C\subseteq D$ of positive cost $P_C=\sum_{p\in C}d(i,p)$, the history in Lemma <a href="#imp:surplus-history" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:surplus-history">21</a> satisfies $$\label{str:conclusion}
 \lambda\le TP_C
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \sum_{p\in C}\alpha_p
 \le \lambda+\left(2-\frac{c(T)}2\right)P_C.$$ The set $C$ need not be a cluster of the algorithm or of an optimal solution.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-str-strictness"><summary>Proof of stmt-str-strictness</summary>

*Proof.* We first turn the replay inequalities into a scalar suffix inequality. After normalizing the cluster cost, two lower bounds on its slack rule out a small total deficit: a tail index would force a large suffix maximum, while an early interval would then carry too much slack. The last step transfers the resulting gap to final budgets.

<a id="str:history-used"></a>
<a id="str:no-overbid"></a>
*Client-removal order and a suffix inequality.* Index the $q=|C|$ clients as $1,\ldots,q$ by their removal step in the fixed replay, breaking ties within an atomic step arbitrarily. This order is used only for the present suffix proof; the final-dual proof in Section <a href="#imp:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:section">7</a> sorts tied removals by final budget. For client $p$, let $Q_p^-$ denote the opened set immediately before its removal step, and let $z_p$ be the phase value at that pre-step state. Thus clients removed in one atomic step have the same $Q_p^-$ and $z_p$. Set $$d_p=d(i,p),\qquad a_p=\frac{z_p}{K}.$$ The imported history properties are $$\label{str:history-used}
 z_p\le t\,d(p,Q_p^-),\qquad
 \alpha_p\le Kz_p,
 \qquad t\,d(p,Q_p^+)\le Kz_p,$$ where $Q_p^+$ is the immediately post-step opened set, together with nonincreasing distances after that step. The pre-step no-overbidding inequality at $i$ is $$\label{str:no-overbid}
 \sum_{j\text{ active}}[z_p-d(i,j)]_+
 +\sum_{j\text{ inactive}}[t\,d(j,Q_p^-)-d(i,j)]_+
 \le\lambda.$$ All sums in Equation <a href="#str:no-overbid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:no-overbid">[str:no-overbid]</a> initially range over $D$.

For $j<p$ define $$r_{jp}=
 \begin{cases}
 t\,d(j,Q_p^-)/K,&j\text{ is already inactive before the step of }p,\\
 z_p,&j\text{ is removed in the same atomic step as }p.
 \end{cases}$$ For fixed $j$, this quantity is nonincreasing as $p>j$ advances. Indeed it is constant within the removal step of $j$; at the first subsequent step, Equation <a href="#str:history-used" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:history-used">[str:history-used]</a> gives $t\,d(j,Q_p^-)/K\le z_j$; and thereafter the opened set only grows. This explains why the value for a tied removal is $z_p$, even though the scaled variable used elsewhere is $a_p$.

<a id="str:suffix"></a>
If $j$ is already inactive, the triangle inequality in the fixed metric extension and Equation <a href="#str:history-used" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:history-used">[str:history-used]</a> give $$r_{jp}\ge \frac{t}{K}\bigl(d(p,Q_p^-)-d(p,j)\bigr)
 \ge a_p-\frac{t}{K}(d_p+d_j)
 \ge a_p-d_p-d_j.$$ For a tied removal the same conclusion follows from $r_{jp}=z_p\ge a_p$. Consequently, with $$A_p=\max_{s\ge p}(a_s-d_s),$$ monotonicity implies, for every $j<p$ and $s\ge p$, $$r_{jp}\ge r_{js}\ge a_s-d_s-d_j,
 \qquad\text{and hence}\qquad r_{jp}\ge A_p-d_j.$$ The contribution of $j<p$ in Equation <a href="#str:no-overbid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:no-overbid">[str:no-overbid]</a> is at least $[r_{jp}-d_j]_+$: this is immediate for a tied removal, and follows from $K\ge1$ for an inactive client. Each $j\ge p$ is active in the pre-step state of $p$, including all tied removals, and contributes at least $[a_p-d_j]_+$. Dropping clients outside $C$ therefore proves $$\label{str:suffix}
 \sum_{j\ge p}[a_p-d_j]_+
 +\sum_{j<p}[A_p-2d_j]_+\le\lambda
 \qquad(1\le p\le q).$$

<a id="str:slack-definition"></a>
<a id="str:slack-envelope"></a>
<a id="str:slack-truncation"></a>
<a id="str:slack-identity"></a>
*Two slack bounds and their exact sum.* Divide all costs, phases, and budgets by $P_C$, retaining their notation. Now $\sum_jd_j=1$ and $\lambda\le T$. Write $D_{p-1}=\sum_{j<p}d_j$ and define $$\label{str:slack-definition}
 E_p=\lambda+1+D_{p-1}+(p-1)d_p-qa_p.$$ Expanding $[x]_+=x+[-x]_+$ in Equation <a href="#str:suffix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:suffix">[str:suffix]</a> yields $$\begin{aligned}
 E_p\ &\ge (p-1)(A_p-a_p+d_p)
       +\sum_{j\ge p}[d_j-a_p]_+
       +\sum_{j<p}[2d_j-A_p]_+ \\
 &\ge (p-1)(A_p-a_p+d_p)\ge0.
 \label{str:slack-envelope}
\end{aligned}$$ Alternatively, first replace $A_p$ in Equation <a href="#str:suffix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:suffix">[str:suffix]</a> by its lower bound $a_p-d_p$, and then expand. This gives $$\label{str:slack-truncation}
 E_p\ge\sum_{j\ge p}[d_j-a_p]_+
           +\sum_{j<p}[2d_j+d_p-a_p]_+
      \ge\sum_j[d_j-a_p]_+.$$ In the sum of $D_{p-1}+(p-1)d_p$ over $p$, the coefficient of $d_j$ is $(q-j)+(j-1)=q-1$. Thus the following identity is exact: $$\label{str:slack-identity}
 \overline E:=\frac1q\sum_{p=1}^qE_p
  =\lambda+2-\frac1q-\sum_{p=1}^qa_p.$$

<a id="str:small-deficit"></a>
<a id="str:jensen"></a>
<a id="str:small-set-mass"></a>
*A small deficit would concentrate neither distance nor slack.* For a contradiction suppose $$\Delta:=\lambda+2-\sum_pa_p<c,
 \qquad c=c(T).$$ Equations <a href="#str:slack-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:slack-envelope">[str:slack-envelope]</a> and <a href="#str:slack-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:slack-identity">[str:slack-identity]</a> imply $$\label{str:small-deficit}
 0\le\overline E=\Delta-1/q<c,
 \qquad 1/q<c,\qquad q>1/c>4/\rho.$$ The function $F(x)=\sum_j[d_j-x]_+$ is convex and nonincreasing. Since Equation <a href="#str:slack-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:slack-identity">[str:slack-identity]</a> also gives $\sum_pa_p\le\lambda+2-1/q<B$, Jensen’s inequality and Equation <a href="#str:slack-truncation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:slack-truncation">[str:slack-truncation]</a> imply $$\label{str:jensen}
 F(B/q)\le F\left(\frac1q\sum_pa_p\right)
 \le\frac1q\sum_p F(a_p)\le\overline E<c.$$ For every index set $J$ with $|J|\le2\rho q$, it follows that $$\label{str:small-set-mass}
 \sum_{j\in J}d_j
 \le |J|B/q+F(B/q)<2\rho B+c
 =\frac1{100}+c<\frac1{50}.$$

<a id="str:tail-large"></a>
*Finding a tail index with both required bounds.* Equation <a href="#str:jensen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:jensen">[str:jensen]</a> shows that fewer than $cq/B$ indices have $d_j>2B/q$, because each such index contributes more than $B/q$ to $F(B/q)$. Equation <a href="#str:small-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:small-deficit">[str:small-deficit]</a> shows that fewer than $10cq$ indices have $E_j>1/10$. Put $r=\lfloor\rho q\rfloor$. The tail of exactly $r$ indices is larger than the union of these two exceptional sets: indeed $$\frac rq\ge\rho-\frac1q>\rho-c>(10+1/B)c.$$ For the last strict inequality, $B>2$ and $\rho<1/400$ give $(11+1/B)c<12\rho^2/100<\rho$. Hence some $s\in\{q-r+1,\ldots,q\}$ satisfies <a id="str:tail-index"></a>$$\label{str:tail-index}
 d_s\le2B/q,\qquad E_s\le1/10.$$ The suffix starting at $s$ has at most $r\le\rho q$ indices, so Equation <a href="#str:small-set-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:small-set-mass">[str:small-set-mass]</a> gives $D_{s-1}>49/50$. Also $(q-s+1)d_s\le r(2B/q)\le2\rho B=1/100$. Rearranging Equation <a href="#str:slack-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:slack-definition">[str:slack-definition]</a> therefore yields $$\label{str:tail-large}
 q(a_s-d_s)=\lambda+1+D_{s-1}-(q-s+1)d_s-E_s
 >\lambda+1.87>\lambda+1.8.$$

<a id="str:early-small"></a>
*An early interval forces too much slack.* Consider the integer interval $$I=\{\lceil\rho q\rceil,\ldots,\lfloor2\rho q\rfloor\}.$$ For $p\in I$, the prefix of length $p-1$ has at most $2\rho q$ indices. Using $E_p,d_p\ge0$ and Equation <a href="#str:small-set-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:small-set-mass">[str:small-set-mass]</a> gives $$\label{str:early-small}
 q(a_p-d_p)=\lambda+1+D_{p-1}-(q-p+1)d_p-E_p
 <\lambda+1.02.$$ Every $p\in I$ precedes $s$: indeed $p\le2\rho q<(1-\rho)q+1\le q-r+1\le s$. Thus $A_p\ge a_s-d_s$, and Equations <a href="#str:slack-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:slack-envelope">[str:slack-envelope]</a>, <a href="#str:tail-large" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:tail-large">[str:tail-large]</a>, and <a href="#str:early-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:early-small">[str:early-small]</a> imply $$E_p\ge(p-1)(A_p-a_p+d_p)>\frac{0.7(p-1)}q.$$ To check the integer rounding, set $x=\rho q>4$. Then $$|I|=\lfloor2x\rfloor-\lceil x\rceil+1>x-1>x/2,
 \qquad p-1\ge\lceil x\rceil-1\ge x-1>x/2
 \quad(p\in I).$$ Consequently $$\overline E\ge\frac1q\sum_{p\in I}E_p
 >\frac{0.7}{q^2}\frac{x}{2}\frac{x}{2}
 =0.175\rho^2>\frac{\rho^2}{100}=c,$$ contradicting Equation <a href="#str:small-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:small-deficit">[str:small-deficit]</a>. We have proved $\sum_pa_p\le\lambda+2-c$.

*Returning to the final budgets.* By Equation <a href="#str:history-used" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:history-used">[str:history-used]</a>, $\alpha_p\le K^2a_p$. As $v\le1$, $K^2-1=2v^2+v^4\le3v^2$, whence $$\sum_p\alpha_p
 \le K^2\sum_pa_p
 \le\lambda+2-c+(K^2-1)B
 \le\lambda+2-c/2$$ by Equation <a href="#str:v-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:v-condition">[str:v-condition]</a>. Multiplication by $P_C$ proves Equation <a href="#str:conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:conclusion">[str:conclusion]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sur:section"></a>
### The low sensitivity case

The goal in this section is to turn a small change in optimum cost under budget reduction into a strict improvement below $2$. We start from the surplus solution, improve it by local search, and use its same replay to bound the opening price. If the bad clusters carry little cost, the weighted swap bound suffices. Otherwise enough of their cost has bounded price for strictness to improve the total while preserving every regular-facility payment.

<a id="sur:parameter-choices"></a>
<a id="sur:size-threshold"></a>
We now fix the parameters used for this part of the algorithm. Let $C_0=C_{1/100}$ be the constant in Theorem <a href="#loc:anchoring" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:anchoring">6</a>; explicitly, the construction there permits $$C_0=10+60000\,2^{3999}.$$ Set $T=200C_0$ and $c=c(T)$ as in Lemma <a href="#str:strictness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="str:strictness">22</a>. Choose a fixed positive rational $v$ in the range of Theorem <a href="#imp:cglss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:cglss">20</a> satisfying $$\label{sur:parameter-choices}
 v\le\min\left\{\frac1{12},\frac{c}{1000}\right\},
 \qquad v^2\le\frac{c}{6(T+2)},
 \qquad \delta=3v.$$ Fix $0<\xi\le1$; the final assembly will choose it smaller still. Choose a fixed integer $N_*$ so large that for every $N\ge N_*$, $$\label{sur:size-threshold}
 N^2 2^{-N}\le v,\qquad 2/N\le c/200.$$ Such an $N_*$ exists because $N^2 2^{-N}\to0$ and $2/N\to0$. Enlarge the common integer surplus bound to satisfy $L\ge\max\{1,N_*\}$ as well as the bound of Theorem <a href="#imp:cglss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:cglss">20</a>. It still has order $O_v(\log(N+2))$, with all preceding constants fixed. In particular, an instance with $k>2L$ has $N\ge k>2N_*$ and obeys Equation <a href="#sur:size-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:size-threshold">[sur:size-threshold]</a>. Instances of the finitely many smaller sizes fall into the small-cardinality branch of the final assembly.

<a id="stmt-sur-low-sensitivity"></a>
<div id="sur:low-sensitivity" class="proposition">

<a id="sur:sensitivity"></a>
<a id="sur:conclusion"></a>
**Proposition 23** (Low sensitivity). *With these fixed parameters, suppose $k>2L$ and $$\label{sur:sensitivity}
 \operatorname{opt}_{k-2L}\le(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ Put $h=k-L$. In polynomial time one can compute a real facility set $S$ of size $k$ with $$\label{sur:conclusion}
 \operatorname{cost}(S)
 \le\left(2-\frac{c}{100}\right)\operatorname{opt}_h
 \le\left(2-\frac{c}{100}\right)(1+\xi)
       \operatorname{opt}_k.$$ The algorithm does not need to test Equation <a href="#sur:sensitivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:sensitivity">[sur:sensitivity]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sur-low-sensitivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-sur-low-sensitivity</summary>

<a id="sur:local-start"></a>
*Proof.* Since $L\ge1$ and $k>2L$, we have $h\ge2$ and $h-L=k-2L\ge1$. Apply Theorem <a href="#imp:cglss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:cglss">20</a> at target $h$, then replay its output using Lemma <a href="#imp:surplus-history" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:surplus-history">21</a> to obtain the real set $U$ and its compatible budgets. In either output branch, $|U|\le h+L=k$. Pad $U$ to size $k$ using members of $F$, and run width-$5$ local search starting there; call its output $S$. Padding is possible because $k\le|F|$, and $$\label{sur:local-start}
 |S|=k,\qquad \operatorname{cost}(S)\le\operatorname{cost}(U).$$ This is polynomial time on the bounded integral metric, as in Lemma <a href="#loc:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:descent">4</a>. Fix an optimum $O$ of size $h$, padding if necessary, and write $P=\operatorname{opt}_h>0$, $C_i=\{p:p\text{ is assigned to }i\in O\}$, $n_i=|C_i|$, and $P_i=\sum_{p\in C_i}d(i,p)$.

*The unmerged small-price branch.* Suppose the output is the unmerged solution at $\lambda=2/N^2$, with at most $h$ facilities. Its payment guarantee is $$(1-\delta)\operatorname{cost}(U)\le\sum_{p\in D}\alpha_p.$$ Every nonempty cluster has $P_i\ge1$ in the positive integral metric, so $\lambda\le2\le TP_i$. Apply Lemma <a href="#str:strictness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="str:strictness">22</a> to all nonempty clusters. Empty clusters contribute no budgets. There are at most $h$ nonempty clusters, and therefore $$\begin{aligned}
 (1-\delta)\operatorname{cost}(U)
 &\le h\lambda+(2-c/2)P\\
 &\le\left(2-c/2+c/200\right)P.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $h\lambda\le2/N\le c/200\le(c/200)P$, because $h\le N$ and $P\ge1$. Finally $$\frac{c}{200}+(2-c/100)\delta
 \le\frac{c}{200}+6v
 \le\frac{11c}{1000}<\frac{49c}{100}
 =\frac c2-\frac c{100}.$$ It follows that $\operatorname{cost}(U)\le(2-c/100)P$, and Equation <a href="#sur:local-start" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:local-start">[sur:local-start]</a> proves the desired conclusion in this branch. This argument uses only the payment bound with its opening term dropped and the strictness just proved.

<a id="sur:payment-dual"></a>
<a id="sur:any-H"></a>
<a id="sur:price-comparison"></a>
<a id="sur:surplus-price"></a>
*Dual comparison with an arbitrary real solution.* It remains to consider the normal branch, whose replay has exactly $h$ paid regular facilities. Put $e=hN\eta$, where $\eta=2^{-N}$. Equation <a href="#sur:size-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:size-threshold">[sur:size-threshold]</a> ensures $e\le v\le vP$. The payment and dual inequalities of Lemma <a href="#imp:surplus-history" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="imp:surplus-history">21</a> are $$\label{sur:payment-dual}
 (1-\delta)\operatorname{cost}(U)+h\lambda-e
 \le\sum_p\alpha_p,
 \qquad \sum_p[\alpha_p-2d(i,p)]_+\le\lambda\quad(i\in F).$$ For any nonempty real set $H\subseteq F$, assign clients to nearest centers of $H$. For its cluster at $i$, the inequality $\alpha_p\le2d(i,p)+[\alpha_p-2d(i,p)]_+$ gives, upon summing, $$\label{sur:any-H}
 \sum_p\alpha_p\le |H|\lambda+2\operatorname{cost}(H).$$ Combining Equations <a href="#sur:payment-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:payment-dual">[sur:payment-dual]</a> and <a href="#sur:any-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:any-H">[sur:any-H]</a> and dropping the nonnegative connection term gives $$\label{sur:price-comparison}
 (h-|H|)\lambda\le2\operatorname{cost}(H)+e.$$ Use for $H$ an optimum with $h-L=k-2L$ centers. Equation <a href="#sur:sensitivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:sensitivity">[sur:sensitivity]</a> and monotonicity of the optimum imply $\operatorname{cost}(H)\le(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k\le(1+\xi)P$, and hence $$\label{sur:surplus-price}
 L\lambda\le2(1+\xi)P+e.$$

<a id="sur:short-price"></a>
<a id="sur:anchoring-scale"></a>
*Short clusters and simultaneous removals.* Set $b=\lambda/8$, and for $i\in O$ let $\operatorname{sep}_i=\min_{j\in O\setminus\{i\}}d(i,j)$. Call $i$ short if $n_i\operatorname{sep}_i<b$; let $N_{\rm short}$ be the number of short centers. Fix a nearest other center $\nu(i)$ for every $i\in O$. Color all centers independently with two colors, and select the short centers of the first color whose designated neighbor $\nu(i)$ has the second color. Each short center is selected with probability $1/4$, so some coloring gives a set $R$ with $$N':=|R|\ge N_{\rm short}/4.$$ For every $i\in R$, $\nu(i)\notin R$. Thus $O\setminus R$ is nonempty, and all clients of $C_i$ can be rerouted to $\nu(i)$ with total increase at most $n_i\operatorname{sep}_i<b$. Keeping all other assignments proves $$\operatorname{cost}(O\setminus R)\le P+bN'.$$ Empty clusters cause no difficulty: their rerouting increase is zero. Apply Equation <a href="#sur:price-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:price-comparison">[sur:price-comparison]</a> to $H=O\setminus R$: $$\lambda N'\le2P+2bN'+e.$$ As $2b=\lambda/4$, we obtain $$\label{sur:short-price}
 \lambda N_{\rm short}\le4\lambda N'
 \le\frac{32}{3}P+\frac{16}{3}e.$$ Equations <a href="#sur:surplus-price" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:surplus-price">[sur:surplus-price]</a> and <a href="#sur:short-price" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:short-price">[sur:short-price]</a> now show explicitly that $$\begin{aligned}
 b(L+N_{\rm short})
 &\le\left(\frac{19}{12}+\frac\xi4\right)P+\frac{19}{24}e
 \le10P.
 \label{sur:anchoring-scale}
\end{aligned}$$ For the last inequality use $\xi\le1$, $e\le vP$, and $v\le1/12$. Since $|S|-h=L$, Equation <a href="#sur:anchoring-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:anchoring-scale">[sur:anchoring-scale]</a> is precisely the scale hypothesis of Theorem <a href="#loc:anchoring" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:anchoring">6</a> with tolerance $1/100$.

<a id="sur:bad-price"></a>
*The bad-cluster count and cost dichotomy.* Apply Theorem <a href="#loc:anchoring" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:anchoring">6</a> to $S,O,b$. We obtain distinct proxies in $S$ for the good centers, with total proxy assignment cost at most $P_g+P/100$, and $m$ bad centers satisfying $$\label{sur:bad-price}
 m\lambda=8mb\le8C_0P.$$ Write $P_b=\sum_{i\text{ bad}}P_i$ and $P_g=P-P_b$. If $P_b\le P/10$, Lemma <a href="#loc:swap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:swap">5</a>, with $u=6/5$, gives $$\operatorname{cost}(S)
 \le\frac65(P_g+P/100)+\frac{17}{5}P_b
 \le1.432P<\left(2-\frac c{100}\right)P,$$ where $c<1$ follows from Equation <a href="#str:constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:constants">[str:constants]</a>.

<a id="sur:summed-gain"></a>
Otherwise $P_b>P/10$. Let $$\mathcal B_T=\{i\in O:i\text{ bad and }\lambda\le TP_i\},
 \qquad Q=\sum_{i\in\mathcal B_T}P_i.$$ For every bad center excluded from $\mathcal B_T$, $P_i<\lambda/T$. Equation <a href="#sur:bad-price" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:bad-price">[sur:bad-price]</a> and $T=200C_0$ therefore imply $$\sum_{\substack{i\text{ bad}\\i\notin\mathcal B_T}}P_i
 \le m\lambda/T\le P/25,
 \qquad Q>P/10-P/25=3P/50>P/20.$$ Every member of $\mathcal B_T$ has a nonempty positive-cost cluster, so Lemma <a href="#str:strictness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="str:strictness">22</a> applies there. For each of the other centers, the cluster version of Equation <a href="#sur:any-H" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:any-H">[sur:any-H]</a> gives $\sum_{p\in C_i}\alpha_p\le\lambda+2P_i$, including empty clusters. Summing over all $h$ centers of $O$ proves $$\label{sur:summed-gain}
 \sum_p\alpha_p
 \le h\lambda+2P-\frac c2Q
 \le h\lambda+\left(2-\frac c{40}\right)P.$$ The full $h\lambda$ in Equation <a href="#sur:summed-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:summed-gain">[sur:summed-gain]</a> cancels the full $h\lambda$ in Equation <a href="#sur:payment-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:payment-dual">[sur:payment-dual]</a>, giving $$(1-\delta)\operatorname{cost}(U)
 \le\left(2-\frac c{40}\right)P+e.$$ To divide by $1-\delta$, note that $$\frac eP+(2-c/100)\delta
 \le v+6v\le\frac{7c}{1000}<\frac{3c}{200}
 =\frac c{40}-\frac c{100}.$$ Thus $\operatorname{cost}(U)\le(2-c/100)P$, and Equation <a href="#sur:local-start" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:local-start">[sur:local-start]</a> proves the first inequality in Equation <a href="#sur:conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:conclusion">[sur:conclusion]</a>. Finally $P=\operatorname{opt}_h\le\operatorname{opt}_{h-L}
\le(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k$ proves its second inequality. All choices made by the algorithm were the imported construction, padding, and local search; $O$, the coloring, and the good/bad partition are used only in the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="asm:section"></a>
### The stability dichotomy and the final algorithm

We now combine the two constructions. The distinction between the cases is used only to analyze the candidates: the algorithm computes all candidates without knowing any optimum cost.

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
#### Choosing the constants

Take $C_0$ from Equation <a href="#loc:main-count-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:main-count-constant">[loc:main-count-constant]</a>, put $T=200C_0$, and let $c=c(T)>0$ be given by Equation <a href="#str:constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="str:constants">[str:constants]</a>. Fix the rational constants <a id="asm:constants"></a>$$\label{asm:constants}
 \gamma=\min\{3/100,c/100\},\qquad
 \xi=\varepsilon_0=\gamma/16,\qquad
 \sigma=\gamma/4.$$ In particular $0<\gamma<1$. The choice of $\varepsilon_0$ fixes the polynomial distance bound in Lemma <a href="#pre:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:rounding">3</a> before the external surplus construction is invoked. Choose $v$ as in Equation <a href="#sur:parameter-choices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:parameter-choices">[sur:parameter-choices]</a>, then choose $N_*$ and the common integer $L$ as in Equation <a href="#sur:size-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sur:size-threshold">[sur:size-threshold]</a> and the paragraph following it. Thus $$L=O(\log(N+2)),\qquad L\ge1,$$ where all hidden constants are fixed independently of the input. There is no dependence of $C_0,T,c,\gamma,\xi$, or $\varepsilon_0$ on $L$. This order also accommodates the fixed coefficient in the rounded distance bound.

<a id="stmt-asm-sensitive"></a>
<div id="asm:sensitive" class="lemma">

<a id="asm:sensitive-ratios"></a>
<a id="asm:separation"></a>
**Lemma 24** (A sensitive cardinality). *Suppose $k>2L$, $0<\xi\le1$, and $$\operatorname{opt}_{k-2L}>(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ Put $\beta=\xi/(4L)$. There is an integer $h'$ with $k-2L<h'\le k$ such that $$\label{asm:sensitive-ratios}
 \operatorname{opt}_{h'-1}\ge(1+\beta)\operatorname{opt}_{h'},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{opt}_{h'}\le(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ For an optimum $O$ of size $h'$, of cost $P=\operatorname{opt}_{h'}$, every comparison cluster satisfies $$\label{asm:separation}
 n_i\operatorname{sep}_i\ge\beta P,
 \qquad \operatorname{sep}_i=d(i,O\setminus\{i\}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-asm-sensitive"><summary>Proof of stmt-asm-sensitive</summary>

*Proof.* All optimum costs in the integral model are positive. Since $\log(1+\xi)\ge\xi/2$ for $0\le\xi\le1$ and $\log(1+\beta)<\beta$, <a id="asm:product-bound"></a>$$\label{asm:product-bound}
 (1+\beta)^{2L}<\exp(\xi/2)\le1+\xi.$$ The product of the $2L$ ratios $\operatorname{opt}_{r-1}/\operatorname{opt}_r$, for $k-2L<r\le k$, is greater than $1+\xi$. Hence at least one ratio is at least $1+\beta$. Choose the largest such index $h'$. Every later ratio is less than $1+\beta$, and their product is at most $(1+\beta)^{2L}<1+\xi$ (the empty product is one). This proves both inequalities in Equation <a href="#asm:sensitive-ratios" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:sensitive-ratios">[asm:sensitive-ratios]</a>.

We have $h'\ge k-2L+1\ge2$. For $i\in O$, reroute all its clients to a nearest other member of $O$ and keep the remaining assignments. This constructs an $(h'-1)$-center solution of cost at most $P+n_i\operatorname{sep}_i$. The first inequality in Equation <a href="#asm:sensitive-ratios" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:sensitive-ratios">[asm:sensitive-ratios]</a> gives $P+n_i\operatorname{sep}_i\ge(1+\beta)P$, proving Equation <a href="#asm:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:separation">[asm:separation]</a>. In particular no cluster of this optimum is empty. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>
#### Candidates on the bounded integral instance

<a id="stmt-asm-integral"></a>
<div id="asm:integral" class="proposition">

<a id="asm:integral-guarantee"></a>
**Proposition 25** (Integral-instance algorithm). *With the constants above, for every $0<\zeta<1$ there is a randomized algorithm which always returns at most $k$ facilities and cost at most $5\operatorname{opt}_k$, and with probability at least $1-\zeta$ returns cost at most $$\label{asm:integral-guarantee}
 (2-\gamma)(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ Its running time is polynomial in $N$ and $\log(2/\zeta)$ for the fixed constants and the fixed polynomial distance bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-asm-integral"><summary>Proof of stmt-asm-integral</summary>

*Proof.* Compute a width-$5$ local optimum $S_k$ of size $k$ and retain it as a candidate. Lemmas <a href="#loc:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:descent">4</a> and <a href="#loc:swap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:swap">5</a> give polynomial time and $\operatorname{cost}(S_k)\le5\operatorname{opt}_k$. Every other retained candidate will also satisfy the budget, and we return the least-cost candidate.

If $k\le2L$, apply Theorem <a href="#comp:recovery" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:recovery">17</a> to $S_k$ with the supplied count bound $M_0=2L$ and failure parameter $\zeta$. For its analysis, take an optimum of size $k$ and declare every cluster bad. Then $m=k\le M_0$, and the proxy condition is vacuous. Recovery gives cost at most $1.97\operatorname{opt}_k$ with probability at least $1-\zeta$. Since $1.97\le2-\gamma$, this implies Equation <a href="#asm:integral-guarantee" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:integral-guarantee">[asm:integral-guarantee]</a>, including the case $k=1$.

Suppose now that $k>2L$. Compute the candidate of Proposition <a href="#sur:low-sensitivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sur:low-sensitivity">23</a> with target $h=k-L$. In addition, for every integer $h'$ with $k-2L<h'\le k$, compute a width-$5$ local optimum $S_{h'}$ of size $h'$ and apply Theorem <a href="#comp:recovery" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:recovery">17</a> with <a id="asm:count-bound"></a>$$\label{asm:count-bound}
 M_0=\left\lceil\frac{4C_0L}{\xi}\right\rceil,
 \qquad\text{failure parameter }\zeta.$$ These calls require only the computed local sets, $M_0$, and $\zeta$. They enumerate their unknown comparison costs internally. All their feasible outputs have at most $h'\le k$ centers.

If $\operatorname{opt}_{k-2L}\le(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k$, Proposition <a href="#sur:low-sensitivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sur:low-sensitivity">23</a> gives cost at most $$(2-c/100)(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k
 \le(2-\gamma)(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ This conclusion is deterministic.

Otherwise, let $h'$ be supplied by Lemma <a href="#asm:sensitive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="asm:sensitive">24</a>, take an optimum $O$ of size $h'$, and put $P=\operatorname{opt}_{h'}$, $b=\beta P$. The local set $S_{h'}$ has no surplus relative to $O$. Equation <a href="#asm:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="asm:separation">[asm:separation]</a> says that no center is short at scale $b$, so the left side of the scale condition in Theorem <a href="#loc:anchoring" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:anchoring">6</a> is zero. With tolerance $1/100$, that theorem supplies distinct good proxies with total assignment cost at most $P_g+P/100$, and $$m\le C_0P/b=C_0/\beta=4C_0L/\xi\le M_0.$$ The recovery call at this particular $h'$ therefore has, with probability at least $1-\zeta$, cost at most $$1.97P\le1.97(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k
 \le(2-\gamma)(1+\xi)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ The algorithm need not recognize this index or its success event: its returned minimum is no worse than this candidate. Only this one call must succeed, so there is no union-bound loss over the other indices.

Both supplied count bounds are $O(\log(N+2))$. There are at most $N$ cardinality choices; the local searches and the surplus construction are polynomial, and the recovery calls have polynomial time by Theorem <a href="#comp:recovery" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:recovery">17</a>. Keeping $S_k$ proves the unconditional factor-five bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-53"></a>
#### General metrics and the expected guarantee

<details id="proof-stmt-asm-integral-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-asm-integral</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#main:theorem" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:theorem">2</a>.* Handle the elementary cases and zero optimum as in Section <a href="#pre:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:section">2</a>. Apply Lemma <a href="#pre:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:rounding">3</a> with the fixed $\varepsilon_0=\gamma/16$ and the algorithm of Proposition <a href="#asm:integral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="asm:integral">25</a>. The successful approximation factor on the original rational metric is at most $$(2-\gamma)(1+\xi)(1+\varepsilon_0)
  =(2-\gamma)(1+\gamma/16)^2
  \le2-\gamma/2.$$ For the last inequality, expand the product: $$(2-\gamma)(1+\gamma/16)^2
 =2-\frac34\gamma-\frac{15}{128}\gamma^2
                  -\frac1{256}\gamma^3
 \le2-\gamma/2.$$ The transferred unconditional bound is $5(1+\varepsilon_0)\operatorname{opt}_k<6\operatorname{opt}_k$.

For the prescribed fixed $a>0$, choose a rational failure parameter $$0<\zeta\le\min\{(N+2)^{-\lceil a\rceil},\gamma/24\}.$$ It has polynomial bit length and $\log(2/\zeta)=O_a(\log(N+2))$ with fixed constants. The least original-cost candidate over all rounding guesses is no worse than the candidate for the correct guess. Hence its probability of exceeding $(2-\gamma/2)\operatorname{opt}_k$ is at most $\zeta\le(N+2)^{-a}$. Since $\sigma=\gamma/4$, this proves the asserted probability bound. Finally, split expectation according to that successful event and use the unconditional cost bound: $$\mathbb E\operatorname{cost}(S)
 \le (2-\gamma/2)\operatorname{opt}_k
              +6\zeta\operatorname{opt}_k
 \le(2-\gamma/4)\operatorname{opt}_k
 =(2-\sigma)\operatorname{opt}_k.$$ Every candidate is a real facility set of size at most $k$. All enumerations and arithmetic have polynomial bit complexity, as established in the reduction, sampling, and completion proofs. This completes the theorem. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The facility count in completion. The reference contains virtual leaves, but the output contains only members of $F$. A chosen facility in a ball replaces its leaf; a nonempty real solution dominates the permanent dummy. The removals are distinct, while additions are set unions, so overlaps and repeated destinations only reduce the count. The figure describes accounting, not a Euclidean embedding or a disjointness assumption on facility balls.",
      "figure_id": "fig:completion-budget",
      "pdf_page": 24,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:completion-budget",
          "exact_quote": "The facility count in completion.  The reference contains virtual\nleaves, but the output contains only members of $F$.  A chosen facility\nin a ball replaces its leaf; a nonempty real solution dominates the\npermanent dummy.  The removals are",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  box/.style={draw=MidnightBlue,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,\n    text width=4.05cm,minimum height=2.05cm,inner sep=5pt,font=\\small},\n  arrow/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},semithick},\n  node distance=7mm]\n\\node[box] (anchor) {\\textbf{Real anchor}\\\\[4pt]\n  $S\\subseteq F$\\\\$|S|=h$};\n\\node[box,right=of anchor] (reference) {\\textbf{Sampled reference}\\\\[4pt]\n  $h-|Q|$ real facilities\\\\\n  $a$ leaves and one\\\\permanent dummy};\n\\node[box,right=of reference] (output) {\\textbf{Real completion}\\\\[4pt]\n  remove $r=m-|Q|$ more\\\\\n  add at most $a+c=m$\\\\\n  keep at most $h$ facilities};\n\\draw[arrow] (anchor)--(reference);\n\\draw[arrow] (reference)--(output);\n\\node[below=8mm of reference,align=center,text width=14.8cm,font=\\small]\n  {$\\displaystyle h-|Q|-r+a+c=h$\\quad before merging repeated choices.\\\\[3pt]\n   Ball destinations: at most $a$; destinations without a ball: at most $c=m-a$.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A flow diagram showing three horizontally arranged rectangular boxes connected by rightward horizontal arrows. The first box contains the centered title \"Real anchor\" followed by \"$S \\subseteq F$\" and \"$|S| = h$\". A horizontal arrow points from the first box to the second box. The second box contains the centered title \"Sampled reference\" followed by \"$h - |Q| \\text{ real facilities}$\" and \"$a \\text{ leaves and one permanent dummy}$\". A horizontal arrow points from the second box to the third box. The third box contains the centered title \"Real completion\" followed by \"$\\text{remove } r = m - |Q| \\text{ more}$\", \"$\\text{add at most } a + c = m$\", and \"$\\text{keep at most } h \\text{ facilities}$\". Below the three boxes, centered horizontally, is the equation \"$h - |Q| - r + a + c = h \\text{ before merging repeated choices.}$\" Beneath that equation is the text \"$\\text{Ball destinations: at most } a\\text{; destinations without a ball: at most } c = m - a\\text{.}$\""
    }
  ],
